A.20. Executing Entity Information

Forestry Commission of Ghana (Climate Change Directorate): The FC is responsible for the conservation and management of forest resources within its jurisdiction. It is mandated to protect, conserve, and manage those resources and the coordination of policies related to forests. The Climate Change Directorate was established in 2007 as a directorate under the FC. The FC is constituted of three main divisions: Forest Services Division (FSD), Wildlife Division (WD) and Forestry Research Division (FRD). The FSD is responsible for forest management, while the WD is the Training Centre and Resource Management Support Centre (with an office in Tamale in the Northern Region). The FRD is responsible for research. The Climate Change Directorate was established as a mandate to manage forestry-sector initiatives related to climate change adaptation and mitigation.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 PAGE 3 OF 83

## Climate Change Problem

### 1. The Northern Savannah Zone (NSZ) is a fragile ecosystem sensitive to the effects of climate change, particularly increasing soil degradation and desertification due to the loss of valuable shade trees.
According to Ghana's forest reference level (FRL), the NSZ has lost 77% of its forest cover between 2001 and 2015. Clearing the removal not only of environmental services but also of the socio-economic benefits (SEBs) that have acted as both a cornerstone of the economy and as a critical safety net for the poor.

### 2. The drivers of this change include conversion to agriculture (at just under 40,000 ha per annum), in particular slash-and-burn farming, which is followed by land abandonment; extensive ploughed and continuous cropping agricultural practices; logging; wood fuel harvesting and charcoal production; firewood collection; and grazing. The cumulative effect of these drivers has led to forest degradation, much of it severe: 100,000 ha per annum have fallen below 15% crown cover and have been converted into grasslands. This has resulted in increased soil erosion, reduced growth of grasses, which, in turn, result in more intense fires in the dry season, frequently causing further degradation and deforestation and preventing regeneration of forest areas already impacted by fire.

### 3. Growing deforestation and removal of trees renders lands in turn agricultural landscapes increasingly prone to desertification. These environmental problems are exacerbated by the effects of climate change. Desertification, climate variability and food security are closely linked through drought, land cover changes, soil degradation, and water availability. For example, farmers in the Upper West Region perceived that rainfall had been decreasing, temperatures increasing, and bush fires, droughts and floods more prevalent.

## Proposed Interventions

### 4. Urgent measures need to be taken now to restore the landscape and shade trees which will in parallel improve livelihoods and environmental resilience and provide real economic benefits for inhabitants.
This project seeks to enhance forest carbon stocks across the landscape by: 1) restoring 200,000 hectares of degraded grasslands and forests under community management in Community Resource Management Areas (CREMAs); 2) restoring 100,000 ha of modified Taungya system plantation in severely degraded forest reserves; 3) establishing a large-scale agroforestry/taungya system/plantation in severely degraded forest reserves; and 4) implementing an integrated monitoring and reporting system. See Table below for a summary of interventions and impacts.

### Table 1 – Summary of Proposed Interventions and Impacts

| Output | Drivers and Vulnerabilities | Direct Impacts |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Output 1: off-reserve, degraded grasslands and forests restored under community management in CREMAs. | Unustainable land use practices; wood fuel and timber; lack of adaptive capacity; firewood collection and charcoal production; grazing; lack of resources available to prevent over-exploitation of forest resources by communities. | 200,000 ha of deforested grasslands restored to forests and managed for sustainable wood fuel and timber; 185,000 ha of modified taungya system (MTS) established in severely degraded lands; CREMAs in place for 200,000 ha; increased income for MTS communities. |
| Output 2: Modified Taungya System plantation and management in forest reserves | | 185,000 ha of modified taungya system (MTS) established in severely degraded lands. |

## Output 3: Degraded Shea forests restored through public-private partnerships

- Decreased length and frequency of fallows; increased harvesting of wood fuels and non-timber forest products
- Economic incentives for shea tree protection
- Reduced fire risk due to the increase in the shea butter value chain
- 175 million shea trees planted with fruiting starting from year 10 and greatly increasing from year 15; 100 million shea-planted and integrated into the landscape; restoration of degraded land, soil fertility, soil reclamation capacity and moisture retention; rehabilitation of water systems which provide multiple benefits as decreased runoff and increased infiltration; improved livelihoods for local producers as they are organized into cooperatives and gain access to markets; increased Shea production, supported by better management practices, resulting in higher yields and increased resilience to negative climate shocks
- Eligibility for cooperative agreements and results based payments under the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility and forest carbon market systems in place (project outputs); safeguards information system developed and operational; forest monitoring system operational

## Output 4: Integrated monitoring system strengthened and REDD+ systems strengthened

- Incompliance with the Warsaw Framework requirement
- Social Analysis for REDD+ under the Environmental Management Framework.

### Climate impacts/benefits

5. These actions will deliver 6.139 million tCO2e in emission reductions and removals over the first seven years of the project's lifetime and 25.24 million tCO2e over 20 years.
- Restoration of trees, decreased deforestation and fire management to reduce the serious effects of land degradation; increased soil moisture; reduction of evapotranspiration and maintenance of soil fertility
- These interrelated social and environmental benefits will contribute to improved ecological and social systems by enhancing the capacity of susceptible groups to sustainably utilize land resources, enable value addition in the utilisation of forest resources and contribute to building an economic base for rural communities. This is consistent with the Government's development agenda (as noted in the National Adaptation Strategy for Climate Change).
- The project will directly strengthen the livelihoods and climate resilience of 100,200 people (78,650 women) through improved access to forest resources; increased income from timber sales; and as a community-based wildlife management platform.
- It will be expanded by the project to include support for community-based conservation initiatives in the parklands. The emphasis is on tree restoration in the parklands and strengthening the value chain for women by improving their access to markets, increasing their participation in decision-making processes more directly and effectively; it is a key component.

8. The project has a gender responsive approach and targets women, who are the key drivers for Output 3. Shea represents between 12%–32% of household income and by-products such as soap and wood fuel provide important additional sources of income for women in the region. A significant increase in the number of women (going to facilitate aggregation and wood-related cost savings) for 20,000 women collectors is significant.

# B.1 Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

## The tropical ecosystems of Ghana’s Northern Savannah Zone (NSZ), an area of about 9 million hectares with a population of over five million people**, are characterized by nearly 400 million shea and other trees growing naturally on traditional farming systems.

The trees are integrated with crops on smallholder farms, providing food security for the region. However, these trees and their associated shrubs are facing a great threat with total forest cover having declined by over 77% between 2001-2016, with the loss of nearly 3 million hectares of natural vegetation (shea, acacia, and other woody species) and degraded forests**. This has resulted in Greenhouse Gas (GHG) emissions of an estimated 3,568 MtCO₂e per annum**.

The main drivers of this deforestation and forest degradation are due to land use by dry pastoralists, commercial logging, and charcoal production. The loss of trees and shrubs due to continuous cropping systems removes an important source of firewood which was harvested from fallow areas in the past. This has led to increased reliance on firewood sourced outside of the agricultural landscape.

## The loss and degradation of woodlands and forests provide significant value to shea and other species pose significant environmental, social and economic benefits in the NSZ. Shea trees and landscapes are important sources of carbon storage and sequestration above-ground and in soils and roots**. Shea is a highly resilient tree species that has been shown to be more resistant than other trees, and is the most significant tree species of these agroforestry paddocks.

It has been retained as a semi-domesticated tree species, providing essential products and ecosystem services for local communities. The shea tree also provides adaptive capacities by making ecosystems more resilient and providing buffering ecologies against climate change (e.g., desertification and land degradation).

## Shea provides a major source of economic livelihood to women in the region. They collect shea from their farms and sell it at market, which is an important income source for them. This increases women’s involvement in the international marketplace and their empowerment. Unlike most agricultural crops that are sold by men, women control the proceeds from shea sales and usually spending it on education, health insurance, and other social services.

According to a study in Northern Ghana**, 90% of women view shea as the major source of income for households**. The increased income from shea sales has also allowed them to add additional food crops to their farms.

## The NSZ had the highest poverty rate among the ecological zones and is the only one that experienced poverty increases in 2016/17 compared to all the other ecologies. These regions often experience droughts, which are more severe than those experienced by the rest of Ghana and the poorest**.

The country’s lowest average annual per capita expenditure by household is in the Upper

## Background
These regions are also the most exposed to the effects of environmental degradation amplifying the effects of climate change. Incidences of drought, soil erosion and floods are the highest in this country**.

### An earlier study showed that South Africa has experienced a significant increase in temperature between 1960 and 2010**.
A later study revealed that excessive droughts in Northern Ghana have contributed to unsustainable high temperatures, soil fertility, poor soil water retention and reduced crop yields. The mean temperature increased by about 4.6°C to 8.2°C by 2080, with the highest increase in Northern Ghana**.

### Against this background, the NSZ must be a target for social, environmental and economic programmes.
The Government of Ghana (GoG), with funding from the Adaptation Fund is implementing a project entitled "Increased resilience to climate change in northern Ghana through the management of water resources". The project aims at reducing the negative impacts of climate variability and unpredictability of water resources, along with the associated negative impacts of these variables on livelihoods. To date, 100 boreholes have been constructed, another 50 under construction. A borehole can provide safe drinking for 300 people. Small dams have been rehabilitated. The project is focused on improving access to water resources and enhancing food security through rain garden seasonings, bee keeping, apiary practices (beehives, bulbous fruits, honey), soya supported by the GoG. The project will also contribute towards climate change adaptation by developing diverse capacities, by documenting and disseminating best practices in the management of water resources and by providing a platform for dialogue on climate change adaptation.
This project is an important complement to the GLELP with its focus on borehole construction and rehabilitation. It will be implemented through the sub-basin committees to manage water resources. Strong acknowledgment of the achievements of the project will be made in the NSZ, which will be used as a model for replication.

### Project implementation
The project is being implemented by the GoG in collaboration with the GLELP. The project implementation ensures that the efforts to manage water in the NSZ can be extended to restoration and enhancement of forest resources. This will provide an integrated approach towards access to clean water, which is a key component of the climate change adaptation project.
For example, the restoration of forest along water bodies within the buffer zones created by the GLELP will ensure that the negative impacts on the environment as the restoration impact in improving water availability for various uses will be realized sooner.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 9 OF 83

## B

### Project objective, outcomes and impacts

#### 26. The forest and woodland areas of the NSZ are found in all off-reserve savannah forests on community land (Output 1) in to forest reserves managed by the State (Output 3); and in the household managed Shea agroforestry parkslands which are also found on community lands (Output 2). All these land categories have been identified as deforestation hotspots.

#### 27. If no technical assistance, institutional and financial barriers to increasing the value of trees and the shea value chain, non-timber forest products and ecosystem services in the landscapes in the NSZ are removed, then the carbon sequestration potential of these areas will be significantly increased because communities will be able to maintain social benefits and financially viable management of lands and natural resources over the long term. The carbon emission reductions that this project will achieve will be due largely to strengthened REDD+ systems and implementation of integrated monitoring system (Output 4).

#### 28. The project advances a paradigm shift away from short-term stop-gap measures to more prolonged, sustainable forest management in the NSZ. This addresses the barriers that currently prevent the restoration and sustainable management for forests and land and achievement of reductions in emissions and strengthened adaptive capacity. These barriers are:

**Barrier 1: Ineffective management of forest in reserves and on community land**

#### 29. NSZ have quasi open access controlled by traditional authorities, generally for a very modest fee or none. Most forest products are harvested in the absence of any forest management system. In an attempt to address this problem, the Community Resource Management Areas (CREMA) provide incentives towards sustainable use and poverty alleviation. Ghana’s Forestry Commission which is mandated with the administration of forests has been working on a model for CREMAs called the CREMA approach which involved legal devolution of management authority to groups of communities in areas where there are no forest reserves. The model was tested in a defined area that includes one or more communities that have agreed to manage their resources in a sustainable manner. This model is now being promoted by the Forestry Commission and forest management framework adopted to phase out exclusive conservation practices to recognize and foster community participation in forest resource management. CREMA has been piloted successfully in Ghana. NGOs (Arochis), civil society organizations, and development partners (USAID) have come in to support the development of CREMAs in the NSZ and some have been set up to focus on renewable energy production.

#### 30. Although the CREMA model would appear to be an excellent base for the development of sustainable savannah forest management systems, none of the CREMAS have fully a developed

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 10 OF 83

## Barrier 2: Inadequate and limited management of national forest reserves in NSZ:

### 32. Outside of CREMA, few of the forest reserves in the NSZ have management plans, and these are largely not implemented. With low timber densities, poor infrastructure and high risks of fire, the management of these forests is difficult. The communities use the reserves for grazing livestock, lumber charcoal and other non-lumber forest products. Hunting using mist to dry season fires has been very popular but it is destructive in the long term. Transient herders use the reserves as pasture for their livestock. This leads to overgrazing and loss of vegetation cover. A recent study by the Government pointing to an average canopy cover across four reserves at just 20%. The modified forestry system suggests that the communities are not managing these forests, but rather using them as grazing areas. This is a barrier to the communities in the landscape, however barriers to setting up the system are the finance to organize community forest management and lack of capacity within the communities to actually accrue so that forests can be restored whilst addressing the drivers of deforestation.

### Barrier 3: Source of sustainable wood supply for charcoal production:

#### 33. The GoG has developed a Nationally Appropriate Mitigation Action (NAMA) for the creation of a sustainable charcoal value chain based on wood plantations and more efficient kilns, but no financial support is available to implement this plan. According to the NAMA, 20% of households use charcoal as their primary energy source, while 37% use firewood. The remaining 43% of households respectively using charcoal and wood fuels as their primary energy source). Demand is expected to grow with population growth (at 2.5% per annum) in the absence of additional supply. There are currently no incentives for farmers to plant trees, nor for smallholder farming systems and from 'un-managed' forest areas with low or no costs or required technology making it difficult for them to access wood fuel. The NAMA proposes that the charcoal sector should demand less. Small payments made to the local chief for rights to harvest fuelwood for charcoal from community forests are not sufficient to cover the cost of planting trees, and the quality of biomass supply within forest areas such as by selective harvesting to strengthen regrowth.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 11 OF 83

## Barrier 4: Threatened flow of benefits from the shea parkslands

The shea value chain is totally dependent on the conservation of the shea parkslands and the replacement and restoration of parkslands that have been lost or degraded. Traditionally, shea fruits are harvested from naturally growing trees. Shea trees have a lifetime of hundreds of years and may be up to 100 m tall. Shea trees can take between 25-30 years to reach maturity after planting between planting and fruiting. In addition, more decades may pass between the time of the first fruiting and the second fruiting. Shea trees are also very sensitive to environmental conditions such as availability of improved seedlings that significantly reduce the gestation period; lack of trampling by livestock; adequate rainfall; and soil fertility. Shea tree planting is a complex process to ensure the true reach maturity. Early trials of plantings have been successful and show fruiting from 10-25 years after planting, depending on the variety. To speed up the process even further (through grafting and other propagation techniques), provide technical support and fill the information gap on the potential economic returns from planting.

## Barrier 5: Lack of confidence in security of supply prevents investment

### Ghana's shea is known on global markets for its good quality, but it represents 17% of the sub-region's total production. The country has been a net importer of shea butter over the past 20 years; in tandem with rising demand, the shea tree stock base has experienced a steady decrease over this period. Increasingly, Ghana is relying on imports to meet its growing domestic and export needs. This has been conducive to substantial investments by the private sector. To create the conditions for expansion of domestic production, it will be necessary to ensure security of supply; to be assured of continuous availability of quality shea butter, security of supply needs to be ensured by restoring shea trees on the landscape on a large scale.

### Ghana shea consumed in export markets is mostly industrially processed (95%), through the following stages:
i. Collection, storage and storage of shea kernels
ii. Kernel aggregation, quality control, transport, and storage
iii. Crushing and extraction of oil from the whole kernel
iv. Fractionation, which separates the oleic and stearic acids from the whole butter
v. Refining, which removes impurities to produce a pure, one-based ingredient
vi. Formulation with other ingredients to obtain final food or cosmetic products
vii. Marketing and retail activities to food and cosmetic consumers around the world

Although shea is consumed in Ghana at all stages of production (crushing), and approximately 5% from stage iv–iv take place in Ghana, as noted above if quality supply of shea is assured, this would require negligible necessity for expanded private sector investment in industrial processing. This will be a win-win situation for the country, providing opportunities for the public sector and farmers to maintaining healthy parkslands with maximized carbon sequestration.

## Barrier 6: Inefficient value-chain for shea

*Further information on charcoal production is provided in Annex IIIg.*


---
**Page 16**
---

38\. More than 600,000 women collect shea fruit each year and process them into shea kernels in Ghana. The majority of them lack market power and have to sell the kernels to middle-men collectors who then sell them on to larger processors. Common local issues for women who collect and process shea into kernels include:

- Limited information on market prices.
- Their processing conditions undermine the value of their produce, leading to low prices in the market.
- Often, women are forced to accept whatever price the collector offers due to their need for immediate income.

Most women operate at the individual or small-scale level and are not organized into groups that can aggregate their produce.

Lack of capacity building for the upscaling of women's groups for business management, warehouse management and direct marketing, and lack of access to information on appropriate technologies for more fuel-efficient processing technologies and for enhanced quality control.


39\. Linked to the shea value chain, the importance of ensuring a gender-responsive approach such that the benefits of the value chain are shared equally among women as the value chain becomes more profitable is required. A barrier to restoring shea trees on the landscape will be the potential issue that this could lead to a reduction in the amount of shea fruit available for women who collect, process and market shea, may prove to be an impediment to the large scale restoration of shea, and sale of shea kernels by women's cooperatives.


40\. A clear focus on women and setting up women's cooperatives is required. The project will address this through a gender action plan (GAP) that includes stakeholder consultation, benefit sharing, and through the gender assessment and action plan to enable address potential conflicts, enhance participation of women in decision-making processes, and ensure that women are able to make decisions related to improved shea tree yield management, participation in the modified fungus system and community forest management.


Barrier 7: Vulnerabilities associated with limited opportunities for off-farm and non-economic activities:

41\. A dependency on food crop farming with very little opportunity for non-farm activities increases the effects of climate change. The share of household income derived from non-food-activities remains stagnant in most rural areas, particularly in the Upper East Region (Upper East) and the Upper West Region (Upper West). For seven to eight months in the year, the majority of the agricultural population in northern Ghana is engaged in food crop farming. In these regions, infrastructure for support off-season agricultural activities are underdeveloped or non-existent. Opportunities for supplementing food and income from the re-named subsistent farming activities during the dry season are limited due to lack of access to markets, poor post-harvest management of forests so that these can be sustainable forms of income in the long term are required. The project will address this by strengthening the existing forest user groups to be strengthened so that they are sustainable in the long term. This will require processes to set up collection and marketing systems for non-food products, development of a set of enforceable rules and benefit sharing which allow revenues from shea and forest products to be generated and investments ploughed back to perpetuate the approach.


Barrier 8: Incomplete data from NSZ for reporting on respecting of safeguards, land use change monitoring and reporting.

### Overview

44. The project has four interrelated outputs that will be implemented through a cross-sectoral, comprehensive and holistic approach driven by communities, women's groups and government institutions. These outputs can be achieved through capacity building, knowledge sharing, impact monitoring and performance contracting. The project will implement the outputs in three series described above in Figure 1. The results are reduced emissions from deforestation through fire and land use management practices, increased carbon sequestration on 471,500 hectares of land, and restoration of degraded forest stocks through shrub restoration. The project will provide adaptation co-benefits for target populations by improving livelihoods in the Northern Savanna Zone of Ghana through improved food security and increasing incomes and options for livelihoods on 471,500 hectares of land in the Northern Savanna Zone of Ghana. The project will also reduce vulnerability to climate change and promote to climate risks for 100,200 direct beneficiaries and indirectly benefits 340,200 inhabitants in the project area.

## 46. The project design recognizes that the landscape of the NSZ contains three main land use cover categories: savannah woodlands and forest on community lands**, sheep-grazing/parklands found mainly on agricultural lands***, and

** Savannah woodlands are the "natural" original forests in the NSZ where agriculture has not been practiced for a considerable period of time. These areas have a relatively open canopy structure with scattered trees and shrubs. The vegetation is dominated by grasses and herbs. There is potential to become woodlands with a nearly closed canopy and a greatly diminished herbaceous layer. However, the vast majority have been modified through agriculture and are now dominated by crops such as rice, maize, millet, sorghum, and groundnuts. These areas are referred to locally as "savannah forests" or "grass savannahs".

*** Parklands are areas of traditional agricultural systems, dating back centuries, that predominated throughout the NSZ. It consists of household managed croplands with long fallow terms, mostly terra-firma (Floodplain). The present day parklands were developed under colonial rule and have been maintained by successive generations of farmers. These areas are characterized by a mosaic of crops and trees. They also purchase land over 10% crown cover of field trees as open forest. All CREMAs consist of a mix of remaining savannah forests and agricultural lands. The project design recognizes that the majority of these parklands have been converted to agriculture in this FP as the "vines landscape". The PC uses the term "savannah woodlands", however the GILRP required a term that distinguished

### 47. A description of each output and activity within each output is presented below. The activities within each output are highly interlinked.

Output 1: Off-reserve, degraded, savannah woodlands and forests restored under self-financing community-managed systems

#### 48. CREMAs are a group of communities that have agreed to work together for the sustainable use of shared forest resources. They are based on the principle of free, prior and informed consent from locally developed consensual constitutions which are backed by the relevant district law and management plans. A certificate of devolution of management responsibility and authority for natural resource management is issued by the District Forestry Officer to each CREMA. Natural Resources provide additional legitimacy for community-based governance and management of the resources.

#### 49. Output 1 will focus on a) the (re)organization and empowerment of women and men in communities in existing CREMAs, and the structuring and empowerment of communities in new CREMAs; b) the establishment of Community Forest Management Committees to manage forest management systems on 205,000 hectares of savannah land plus 220,000 hectares of deforested grassland; c) the provision of training for women and men in communities in existing CREMAs on the requisite knowledge and skills; and d) support for enterprise development through the integration of investment components in forest management plans that create optimal levels of revenue generation to ensure equitable job creation, income generation and ensuring financial sustainability of the CREMA.

#### 50. Output 1 will be implemented by FC (Forest Service Division) in collaboration with CREMA secretariats and supported by Community Resource Management Committees which include traditional Authorities. Various institutions will be involved through the implementation of activities.

### Activity 1.1 Structure and empower communities for savannah forest management

#### 51. The ten candidate CREMAs sites are presented in Table 2. ## As community forests are protected, managed and used, they will provide a stream of benefits and income over time from the various locations in CREMA lands.

All CREMA stakeholders must agree on how to share these benefits and income. These benefits include both financial and non-financial needs. Benefits usually include financial as well as non-financial resources, including payments at the point of sale for charcoal and fuelwood, payments for carbon credits, payments from community development projects and scholarship funds. These criteria will be included in the constitution of the CREMA. The distribution of benefits and income is determined by consensus amongst the community members/producer groups (including those from more marginalized groups, such as women, youth, and men).

### As revenue is generated, from the second year, off-cuts of wood for fuelwood and charcoal, transport of charcoal to market, a small percentage (up to 7%) of the revenue from revenue generating activities that the CREMA will be undertaking will be used to capitalise a community fund. This fund will be managed by a group of elected representatives from different charcoal clubs and fuelwood as well as potentially from partnerships brokered (through Output 2) for private sector investment in the charcoal and fuelwood value chain. The revenue generated from these activities can also be considered as well as revenues from MTS agricultural surplus in Output 3. This community fund will be used to finance the CREMA’s operations, including the salaries of elected CREMA members, a district assembly environmental officer and a traditional authority representative. Financial oversight and auditing of accounts by the CED will be clear mechanisms for financial transparency and accountability. The CED will also act as a third-party monitoring entity included in the structure.

Municipal and District Assemblies representatives also form part of the CREMA executive bodies that contribute into the CREMA constitution which spells out the magnitude of activities and responsibilities). These bodies will contribute financing towards the CREMA community fund. The amount to be contributed by each body is determined based on its share of the district principal or District Assemblies after community development needs are prioritised. As they have representatives in the CREMA executive, they can also provide technical advice and support. Depending on the business venture being implemented and its alignment with the district or municipal priorities, the municipality or district assembly may be required to contribute into the CREMA enterprises. The assemblies have provision to contribute about 10-25% of the running cost of the CREMA from their budgetary allocation.

### The CREMA community will appoint a group to manage the community funds. These funds will be used for any activity such as a CREMA manager, accountant or forester (which the community will decide on). The fund should increase over time for when it can then pay for services such as forest management and maintenance of the forest. The fund could also be used for any other “green” activity that will contribute to maintaining the forest cover, protecting and restoring the biodiversity.

The utilisation of the funds will be subject to investment criteria that will be developed by the CRAC, following best practice with oversight and guidance from FC, Business Advisory Centre and the project management unit.

### A secondary, more local and smaller scale revolving fund is also envisaged whereby, for example women’s groups or youth groups (or a purely voluntary basis) want to become a member of a group where they contribute an agreed amount according to a specific timeframe, such as every quarter. The funds will be used to provide small business loans and/or conditional loans to those participating members for their livelihood activities of their choosing.

61. The land use plans developed by the community in the CREMAs will be informed by appropriate participatory and gender responsive resource assessments or forest inventories, application of silvicultural methods (as well as for fuel wood production), development of fire management strategies on a range of forest types and analysis of value chains.

A UNDP NAMA study on the carbon value chain estimated that the Northern region contributes 33% to total national emissions from land use change and forest degradation, while the Upper West 4%. Of the total, 14% is utilized in the NSZ CREMA operations aim to meet local demands substantially for fuelwood and charcoal. ## Output 2: Degraded Shea parklands restored through public-private partnerships

73. Output 2 will focus on enhancing carbon stocks through the restoration of 100,000 ha of degraded and underutilized shea parklands and increasing climatic resilience through economic incentives for shea tree protection at the local, national, and international levels. GCF funds and co-financiers will support the implementation of this output by providing technical assistance to farmers in selecting and quicker yielding varieties of shea and 400,000 other high value trees within agroforestry systems, increasing market access for shea products through a public-private partnership with Ghana Shea (GSA) and commercial contracts between members of the Global Shea Alliance (GSA) and women’s cooperatives**. Increased income will be recorded by tracking the prevailing market price in June July and August each year to determine the price that the farmers/ cooperatives sell their shea to the buyers.

74. A final component of this output is to establish a multi-stakeholder process to undertake analysis of the northern Ghanaian zone and engage in a multi-stakeholder process to determine and implement the long-term vision for a climate-smart Shea industry in Ghana. Activity 2.3 will consist of targeted engagement with key stakeholders, including government agencies (MOFA, FCA, GCAA), NGOs and provide inputs for a Ghana’s shea sector strategy. Best practices, in terms of parkland management and restoration, will be shared across the region. The GSA aims to develop a model to upscale the project in Ghana as well as other countries in the region. The GSA aims to develop a larger program in the Shea producing zones across West Africa (Burkina, Benin, Mali, Togo, Nigeria, Côte d’Ivoire) with the aim of increasing the economic and social benefits on economic, social (especially for women) and environmental benefits obtained across wider landscapes.

#### Activity 2.1 Restore and sustainably manage the shea parklands

##### 78. Activity 2.1 will begin with the development of a communication and extension plan based on key messages that will guide the restoration of the restoration of shea in the parklands that will be disseminated across the project zone.

##### 79. Nurseries for the production of seedlings will be set up and run by women's cooperatives set as part of this Activity 2.2. The women's groups will be involved in the nursery production and out planting of shea trees. They will also be trained on the following: the field planning schemes, techniques for planting and care of the young trees and other technical aspects such as tree protection against grazing and other disturbance factors. The trees will be protected when they are older from this activity.

##### 80. Nursery and planting methods for shea will include those from latest trials that reduce the fruit production period to 2-4 years (as opposed to 15 years in the wild). Examples of new methods include: use of seedlings with a higher germination rate, use of improved techniques for seed propagation and growth cultures. These methods combined with fire management and efforts to increase pollination will be employed to reduce the waiting time for shea fruits to appear on the trees.

1) Depending on the weather type, shea can begin to fruit from between 7-20 years old but does not reach its peak yield until aged 40 or 50 years. Trees may live and sequester carbon for 200 to 600 years. Methods for reducing the period of time before flowering and fruiting are being developed in Burkina Faso, where the shea tree is known as "Koume". The project site with its CREMA in the NSE was conducted by the Cocoa Research Institute at the Bobo-Subra station. This has resulted an increased number of seedlings per kg of seeds sown and a higher germination rate (80%). In addition, the use of improved techniques for seed propagation, seed fruiting may start from 2-5 years (Seeds are raised in nurseries before transplanting after 1 year). In Burkina Faso, tree hives have been used to increase pollination and improve yields. The project site with its CREMA in the NSE has fire management and efforts to increase pollination will be employed to reduce the waiting time for shea fruits to appear on the trees.

2) Shea is primarily pollinated by bees, but there is a certain level of self-pollination. The main pollinators are honeybees and several species of wild bees. In Burkina Faso, where the project site with its CREMA in the NSE was conducted, an increase in pollination from 10% to 35% was observed. A significant increase in fruit production (from 20 kg/ha to 40-60 kg/ha) and a decrease in fertilization percentage and number of honeybee colonies within radii of 900 and 1000 m was observed. 81. In addition to the shea trees, another 480,000 trees will be planted (with 83% of these trees reaching maturity). The remaining 17% in the enrichment plantation could include Adansonia digitata, Mitragyna oleifera, Parkia biglobosa, Faidherbia albida, Anogeissus leiocarpus; Terminalia spp., Balanites aegyptiaca. Pterocarpus erinaceus, Diospyros mespiliformis, Combretum molle, and Combretum zeyheri, as well as the indigenous species Combretum molle33. These species will be considered and ranked based on preferences by the women and men of the communities, their ability to provide income and food security, provision of firewood, dyes for materials, traditional drinks such as pitoh, leaves for forage, fruits, medicinal and provision of protection against soil erosion. The trees will be planted in areas that are not used for farming fields and fallow areas, or near homesteads in the open plantations within the CREMA areas for timber.

82. This community, with the guidance and support of the NGOs that have expertise in agroforestry will decide on the trees to be planted to ensure they meet their specific needs; whether in terms of alternative income generation, woodfuel and food production, or agricultural benefits and improved soil fertility. The decision-making process is a participatory one involving all stakeholders. This ensures oversight by the community and increases the likelihood that they will be valued and taken care of. The trees will be planted in areas that are not used for farming fields and fallow areas and planned on individual and community land, depending on community preferences, ensuring that tree ownership rights are respected. It is important to note that although the majority of the trees will be planted, however usage rights are different depending on the trees. Intended beneficiaries should be involved in the decision-making process.

It also outlines the level of care is needed. The manual also addresses policy and capacity gaps to be addressed during implementation.

84. To maximize survival rates in the early stage, the Global Shea Alliance and partners will embed survival performance indicators into the public-private partnership contracts, as well as engage the private sector in the monitoring and evaluation process for tree planting and care. All parties will be responsible for the tree survival, not just the farmers. Together, they will define which monitoring and support schemes are the most appropriate.

#### Activity 2.2 Strengthen value chain for shea processing

85. This activity ensures that there are significant economic incentives for tree protection and maintenance, i.e., that the connection between trees and income is strong. Indeed, shea trees are not strictly a wild species, but a semi-domesticated one, selected and protected by farmers because of its value. The project will ensure that this value is recognized in order to increase the economic value of shea trees for producing communities and increase shea-released income by 30% over five years.

86. The activity will create a long-term relationship between buyers and communities, which will encourage the continued investment into livelihoods and the environment. To help reinforce the crucial role that women play in the production process, the GSA also aims to strengthen the economic value of shea trees for producing communities and increase shea-released income by 30% over five years.

87. This output will build public private partnerships between these upstream producers and the downstream processors. The project will also ensure that the shea value chain is linked back to global ingredient suppliers, cosmetic companies and other international buyers who are investing in market development for shea products. This will ensure that the economic benefits of a shea generated over time, as the impact of landscape restoration takes effect. All the largest ingredient manufacturers and major retail brands buy shea-based ingredients from are engaged as stakeholders in the project.

88. Overall, the capacity and income of 20,000 women are expected to increase through the various improvements made along the value chain (e.g., more efficient collection of shea, access to new technologies, aggregation in warehouses and direct sales to processors for higher prices). Specific activities include: training on market development; establishment of a cooperative; financial subsidies to cooperatives; equipment provision such as jute sacks or scales; trainings related to cooperative management, business development, aggregation, quality, and conservation. This will allow women to be more competitive in the shea value chain.

89. As part of the GSA sustainability program, the women's cooperatives are encouraged to engage in alternative income-generating activities, leveraging their warehouse, shea income, and other partnerships. These activities can be farming, soap-making, bee-keeping etc. The shea cooperatives will also be encouraged to apply for microfinance loans from the CREAMA or the regional banks. The community fund or trust fund will enable a transition from the current grant-based sanctions.

93\. The NZL contains 56 forest reserves covering an area of 638,800 ha as well as two protected areas covering 512,200 ha of which 455,700 ha are in More National Park (NP), one of the country's flagship conservation and tourism attractions. The reserves' degraded/deforested parts will be restored through a Modified Taungya approach.

94\. MTS plantations have been tested across Ghana and are promoted by the FC in order to attain their objectives. These include: increasing forest cover, improving soil fertility, providing products of forestry management and allowing access to land for agriculture. Farmers identified from studies from 2013-2015 as being interested in MTS were found to be more likely to participate if they had signed up contracts, lack of consideration of farmer preferences for crops and NTFPs, and farmers' trust in the FC.

95\. Under this Output, the FC will establish 18,600 hectares of MTS plantations on deforested portions of 12 identified forest reserves and will promote fire management in both savannah forests and grasslands. The approach will be based on lessons learned from previous experiences and best practices from the FC's experiences and will include benefit schemes that incentivises communities to participate in the planting of mixed stands of species. FC will sign contracts with the communities, manage the plantations through a participatory process and provide technical assistance. In addition to co-financing, FC will also establish another 7,000 hectares of plantations with hired labour.

96\. Revenues from the MTS plantations will be shared by FC and the farmers in line with the benefit sharing arrangement as set out under the FRL. The benefit sharing arrangement is based on individual members make up on agricultural products). Some results under this output will also bring adaptation to climate change, including: increased income and food security through diversification of risks; ecosystem-based services restored or through (the) increased income and livelihood made possible by benefit shares; a greater share in the benefits from the MTS plantations; and improved access to MTS management structures, to ensure women and men equally benefit from such outcomes of the work, and balance this also with the work that women do in output 2, which will in turn help to break down gender-based inequalities in the forestry sector.

### Activity 3\.1 Structure MTS communities with clearly defined contractual and benefit sharing arrangements

97\. The MTS approach reduce climate-related vulnerabilities by increasing income and food security through diversification and spreading of risks. This activity will support a formal consolidation of information on best practices recently gathered for FC, and the application of these during project inception. The FC will also work with communities to develop and implement community guidelines for perfecting the approach during implementation, with a strong focus on fire protection and management. In addition, 10% of the areas of the reserves classified under the FRL as grasslands will be converted to MTS plantations.

98\. Through a participatory and evidence-based process, the FC will select the targeted communities and with them set up the MTS structures and arrangements, leading to the signing of contracts with farmers. The benefit sharing arrangement is based on individual members make up on agricultural products with fast growing species to provide woodfuel from officials will be integrated into the forest management approach. By design, the taungya farmers would be organised into smaller groups in order to ensure that they are able to access the services provided by the Forestry Commission and Ministry of Agriculture will be organised for farmers periodically. Common inter and intra-group meetings shall be created to enhance effectiveness and efficiency of the training. This gives the 13,000 taungya farmer the platform to enhance their work and discuss opportunities and challenges.

99. The Timber Industry Development Division of the Forestry Commission regulates the utilization and processing of all timber and timber products. The profits from the wood-based industries will be shared with communities through the Microfinance Trust Scheme (MTS). 100. A variety of tree species, including local hardwood species, will be used for the establishment of MTS plantations in the targeted communities. The plantations will be monitored and supervised and benefit from participatory adaptive management reviews. The lead partners will be the Forestry Commission with the Taungya farmers.

102\. This activity will focus on fire management, prevention and control for the protection of MTS plantations. Maximum use of early burning of the savannah forests will be done to create fire breaks and reduce the risk of forest fires in the dry season. These risk reduction measures provide both mitigation and adaptation benefits to target populations. It will also contribute towards the restoration of degraded MTS plantations and for the restoration of savannah woodlands. As noted, training of fire brigades and implementation of fire management plans covering the entire reserve areas will be undertaken.

103\. Following crown closure, the individual landowners farmers still have to maintain their sections of forest reserves in good condition. This can only be achieved if they are able to retain at least 40% of the final harvest as per current benefit sharing arrangements determined by the FC. This has been a major challenge for the implementation of the Forest Code. The government is committed over such a long period without cash returns. The failure of one farmer to maintain sections of forest reserves will have an impact on other farmers in the same community and also with community structures for fire control and management, and benefit sharing arrangements for harvests revenues need to be modified accordingly.

104\. Ultimately, the development of community structures will be funded directly for fire protection and fire management out of FC revenues from the harvest of MTS plantations. GCF funding is needed to make the initial investments. Once these are made, the programs can continue on a self-sustaining basis. The funds will be managed by the Forest Service in collaboration with community level and capitalised by shea income, surplus agricultural products and community forests.

Sub-Activities will include:

3\. 3\.1 Develop fire management, prevention and control plans for the protection of MTS plantations and for the restoration of savannah woodlands

3\. 3\.2 Build capacity of fire brigades and support implementation of fire management plans.

105\. This output has a two-fold objective: 1) enable the completion of Ghana’s Wanawo Framework architecture and 2) monitor implementation and outcomes of the project ensuring that safeguards are being respected. The project will support the development of an integrated monitoring system and seeks to implement action which will result in reducing emissions and enhancing removals from the forest sector. This output will also seek to strengthen the capacity of the Ghana Forest Service for the operationalization and strengthening of the National Forest Monitoring System (NFMS), and inform the establishment of a National Forest Information System (NFIS) and a Geographical Information System (GIS) for reporting on how Ghana is addressing and respecting safeguards. Methods and protocols will be developed to ensure that data from the project are integrated into the content of the Ghana Cocoa Forest REDD+ Programme (GCFRP). These will be used to enable data and inputs to be reported in accordance with the UNFCCC guidelines. The data and information from the GSLERP will be integrated with the National Climate Data Hub within the Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) to facilitate effective reporting to the UNFCCC.

## Activity 4.2 Monitor, report and verify greenhouse gas emission reduction targets

### 111. The aim of establishing a forest reference level (FRL) is to construct a baseline upon which the information on the current status of GHG emissions from forests in Ghana can be established and the benefits from REDD+ interventions would be measured against. FRL and NFMS are essential tools for demonstrating transparency and accountability credentials of Ghana's REDD+ work and as such, they will be developed under the same framework. The Technical Assessment Report (TAR) for the GFGRP and subsequent national FRL was finalized in 2016 with the draft submitted in 2017 to UNFCCC. The TAR has been reviewed by the National REDD+ Task Force and is now undergoing a process for one year. The final version of the Technical Assessment Report has been officially published in August 2018. The FRL will be developed based on the TAR, ensuring its consistency with the National Green House Gas Inventory Report which will be included in the technical assessment report to be submitted to UNFCCC. The NFMS will also adopt some methodologies for the calculations of forest degradation and assessment of accuracy of land use change maps.

### 112. The standard operating procedures and guidance document that cut out the step-by-step methodology for the development of the FRL and MRV factors for the FRL and MRV will inform the development of the sub-national FRLs for GSLERF and the improved monitoring, measurement and reporting (MMR) framework for the national NFMS. This will ensure consistency in the technical process. This will contribute to the step-wise improvement of the national FRL.

### 113. There has been investment both from the public and private sector in Ghana's National Forest Monitoring System (NFMS). The development of the NFMS is a collaborative effort between government institutions and other players in the REDD+ sector. As part of Output 4, efforts shall be made to establish an integrated NFMS that will provide a common platform for all stakeholders involved. To this effect, a national forest monitoring framework document has been developed which provides a road map for the establishment of the integrated NFMS.

### 114. The focus will be to harmonize and bring together the various components housed in different institutions and agencies to meet the reporting requirements: regular tracking and reporting of GHG emission reductions (with links to the SIS) or transparent communication of GHG emissions from forests. This will also ensure that all stakeholders involved in NFMS Ghana is to be in a position to present a proposal to receive further results based payments under the UNFCCC framework. The development of an integrated NFMS will provide a common platform with transparent and accurate information about resources under their jurisdiction and third-party verification of reported activities. GSLERF will provide the elements to complete the NFMS platform.

### The activity will build on the relevant systems in place to implement the provisions of the EU-IFC VOLUNTARY PARTNERSHIP AGREEMENT (VPA), a process which began in 2009. A Wood Tracking System (WTS) has been established by the FC and RMSSC, with support from GEF/UNDP. The agreement sets out the comments and actions of both parties to tackle illegal logging among others. The VPA is an international agreement between EU countries and developing countries that have evolved over the period with close collaboration and consultations in making decisions that relate to timber trade. The VPA has been signed by Ghana, and the FC has been actively involved since 2013. The VPA has set out the standards for enforcement of regulations against illegal timber harvesting and trade. The WTS has provided a means of tracking wood from the source to the final product which makes it easier to identify legal wood, verification Auditing and eventual acquisition of EUCUT license to export. It also provides a mechanism for FC staff planning.

With regards to leakage and displacement of emissions reductions, the risks have been considered in the draft environmental and social management framework in Annex 6b. The planned actions are expected to address these risks through the implementation of appropriate measures under legal and regulatory frameworks including tenure, support and ownership among stakeholders, and potential compensation for displaced communities. These measures will be further strengthened fully embedded in the project activity through fire management by community and the FC. Factors such as climate change, poverty, lack of access to markets, and weak governance are some of the drivers of activities described in outputs 1.2 and 3. These address the underlying and indirect drivers of deforestation, forest degradation, and barriers to sustainable management, conservation, enhancement of forest carbon stocks and land use change rather than only addressing direct drivers at specific locations.

The Forestry Commission has established REDD+ safeguard focal point in the districts and their role is to ensure that all stakeholders are involved in the implementation of the project. They will work with the safeguard focal points to implement specific management measures. In addition, working

B.4. Implementing arrangements

119. The relationship between the GCF and UNDP as Accredited Entities will be regulated by the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) and the Funded Activity Agreement (FAA). The project will be implemented in accordance with the POPP Framework for Implementation Arrangements (NIW), to the Standard Basic Assistance Agreement between UNDP and the GoB, and the policies and procedures outlined in the UNDP POPP (see <https://popp.un.org/sites/default/files/POPPOpenAccess.pdf>).

119. The Implementing Partner (or Executing Entity - EE) for this project is the Forestry Commission (FC). The FC will be responsible for implementing the project and ensuring that the outputs are of high quality, and it is the EE for all outputs. It is accountable to UNDP for managing the project to deliver the planned outputs in a timely manner within budget constraints. The FC will ensure that the project is managed and accurately on progress report and risk against agreed workplans and results frameworks. In accordance with the POPP Framework for Implementation Arrangements, the FC will maintain and documenting maintenance and evidence that describes the proper and prudent use of project resources and the achievement of project outputs and outcomes as applicable.

120. These relationships are summarized through the following legal agreements:

i. between GCF and UNDP (as Accredited Entity) Funded Activity Agreement.
ii. between UNDP (as Accredited Entity) and the Forestry Commission (as Executing Entity): Project Document; and
iii. between FC as Executing Entity and GSA (as Responsible Party). This agreement will be set up by the legal unit of the FC. FC's protocols will be followed and the agreement will be in the form of a Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) or Letter of Agreement (LoA) as outlined in Annexes 2.1 and 2.2. It will work in conjunction with the private sector entities to deliver the outcomes related to the project.

## Decision-Making Process

121. The project will be governed by a Project Board which is responsible for making management decisions by consensus when required, including the approval of project plans and revisions. It reviews progress reports on project implementation, including progress reports and the combined delivery report. It will ensure the implementation of management plan for project risks identified and oversee the project implementation, reviewing and ensuring compliance with the approved project plan.

122. Board decisions are made in accordance with standards to ensure management for development results: best value for money, fairness, integrity, transparency and effective international cooperation. The Project Board reviews and approves the project document and its approved multi-year workplan and it decides on project changes through appropriate revisions; it ensures that required resources are committed, arbitrates any conflicts in the project, and negotiates any issues between UNDP and the National Project Director. It also monitors the performance of the project and responsibilities of the project manager and any delegation of project assurance responsibilities. In case a consensus is not reached within 30 days, a final decision shall rest with UNDP's Resident Representative or staff member delegated by him/her.

123. The Board will meet at least twice in a year; in addition to ad-hoc/emergency meetings called by the National Project Director.

* The Executive: The CEO of the FC, who will chair the Project Board and represent project interests.
In addition, the National Project Director, who will be appointed by FC and will be responsible for overall direction, strategic guidance and delivery of project outputs (position non-remunerated with GCF funds).
* The Senior Supplier: UNDP in its capacity as GCF accredited entity will represent the interests of the project beneficiaries.
* Senior Beneficiary: Representing the interests of those who will ultimately benefit from the project. They are expected to provide inputs and feedback on the project design, implementation and results from the perspective of project beneficiaries. It includes GSA (representing the private sector partners) and representatives of other relevant government agencies such as NDC, UNDP Ghana Sub-Working Group, and other relevant government MAs including MOFA, MNLR, Ministry of Finance, EPA, and Ministry of Environment, Science, Technology and Innovation (MESTI).

## Management Structure

124. The project will be hosted by the Climate Change Directorate in FC, which will provide staff for overall coordination and monitoring of the project.
A Project Management Unit (PMU) will be established to manage the day-to-day operations of the project.
Led by a Project Manager (PM) that will be selected through a competitive selection process, the PMU will execute project activities, including day-to-day operations of the project, and the overall management of the project. The PM is responsible for ensuring that all deliverables are run on the project on a day-to-day basis within the constraints laid down by the Project Board.
The Project Manager's role includes but not limited to: (i) Ensuring that all deliverables required by the GCF and UNDP, have been completed and submitted to UNDP. The Project Manager’s prime responsibility is to ensure that the project produces the results specified in the project proposal; (ii) Be responsible for overall PMU and within the specific budget allocated time and cost.

125. The overall strategy of FC is to maximize impact through a multi-tiered approach at all levels, especially implementation of Outputs 1 and 3.
Gender balance will also be sought amongst project staff.
All Divisions of the Commission have Regional and District offices spread across the length and breadth of the country.
At the Regional and District Level, the various arms of the FC – Forestry Division (FD), Forest Resources Management Division (FRMD), Community Development Division (CDD), Forestry Commission Training Centre (FCTC) and Resource Management Support Unit (RMSU) are all present to ensure that forest resources are protected from encroachment by human activities, protect and sustain forests and ensure management, technical support and advice and monitoring functions are in place.
These include a Regional Manager, two Assistant Regional Managers per region, District Managers, Range Supervisors, Forest Rangers, Community Forestry Officers, Assistant District Managers and Forest Guards. More women are being engaged to various positions ranging from Ranger Directors, Regional Managers, District and Assistant District Managers and Range Supervisors.

126. UNDP provides a three-tier oversight and quality assurance role involving UNDP staff in Country Offices and at regional and headquarters levels.
The quality assurance role supports the Project Board and PMU in ensuring that project activities are being implemented as per approved functions.
The role ensures appropriate project management milestones are managed and completed.
The project assurance function is carried out by a Project Assurance Officer (PAO) who reports directly to the GCF. UNDP is required to deliver GCF-specific oversight and quality assurance services including:
(i) Day-to-day oversight supervision,
(ii) Oversight of project completion and end, and
(iii) Oversight of project reporting.
The PAO will be supported by a team of technical experts who provide funding and/or technical expertise to the project (designing, developing, facilitating, procuring, implementing). In addition, the Government of Ghana may request UNDP to provide

## Engagement of the private sector

### 127.
The activities to be co-financed by private sector partners will remain their own responsibility and all funds would be managed directly by themselves. No funds will be co-mingled with the GCF funds being disbursed for this project. The private sector partners will be responsible for liaising with the private sector co-financers on their funded sub-activities, monitoring their performance, and ensuring that there is synergy and complementarity between these sub-activities and those funded by the GCF.

### 128.
GSA will also represent the private sector partners in the Project Board and ensure that their interests in promoting a sustainable, profitable and competitive sheep industry are not compromised.

### 129.
The private sector co-financing for Activity 2.2 will be disbursed through a joint proposal mechanism where the GCF funds will be used to finance the establishment of a public-private partnership (PPP) for the procurement of equipment and location of activities. These activities include warehouse construction, distribution of equipment, training related to cooperative management, business development, aggregation of kettles in warehouses, transportation of kettles from production sites and distribution to market locations. The private sector will implement these activities directly within a public-private partnership framework. These proposals will be developed by the private sector partners with representatives from the GSA, UNDP. Once approved, companies will implement the agreed activities and report on their progress to the Project Board. The GCF funds will be disbursed in line with the NGO to implement the GCF-funded activities. This is basically a vendor relationship (contract) with the GCF as the funder (based on payment management principles and the modality that will be used is a payment for achieved results/deliverables).

### 130.
Evolving farmer organizations to be capable of aggregation, warehousing and direct sales will require continuous monitoring and evaluation. A supply chain model will be developed with mid-term evaluation to monitor change in market structure and monitor real change in smallholder systems.

## Describe the experience and track record of the FC and EDs with respect to the activities (sector and country/region) that they are expected to undertake in the proposed project

### 131.
The Forest Service Division (FSD), under the Forestry Commission (FC) of Ghana. It is responsible for the regulation of utilization of forest and wildlife resources, including fire management and control, the conservation and management of those resources and the coordination of activities with other government agencies such as the Ministry of Lands and Natural Resources (MLNR). The FC is constituted of three main divisions: Forest Services Division (FSD), Forestry Research Division (FRD) and Forestry Education and Training Division (FETD). Other specialist units under the FC are the Training Centre and Resource Management Support Centre (with an office in Tamale in the NSC). The FC has decentralized offices at the regional and district levels.

## The FC has vast experience in managing and implementing projects and programs relating to forest land use change, biodiversity conservation, wildlife management, natural resource and environmental governance among others.

The FC, with support from Global Environmental Facility (GEF), implemented the High Forest Biodiversity Conservation Project (HFCBP) in Indonesia. This project was designed as a pilot for REDD+ implementation in Indonesia. The HFCBP intended to increase the ecological security of globally significant biological resources. Between 2009 and 2013, the FC has been implementing the HFCBP with funds from a number of sources including the EU, Switzerland, DFID, World Bank, the Netherlands, France with a total amount of US$45M to ensure predictable and sustainable financing for the forest sector in Indonesia.

The FC also implemented the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (FCPF) project. The FCPF supported the FC with 12M euros to implement the Wildlife Management Project for the rehabilitation of fire-degraded forests to recover lost economic, social and environmental benefits to the rest of the transitional zones.

## The FC is currently implementing a large portion (US$30 million) of a USD 50 million Forest Investment Program (FIP) being funded by the Climate Investment Funds. Which is inter alia putting key REDD+ projects in place in Indonesia, Brazil and Peru. A significant proportion of the FIP is to reduce greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions from deforestation and forest degradation, while reducing poverty and improving livelihoods.

The FC has completed the REDD+ Readiness Phase funded by CFCF (USD 8.6M) and signed an emissions reductions purchase agreement with the Carbon Fund for the GCFRR. The FC signed an agreement with the World Bank to provide technical assistance to governments in developing public and private sector carbon projects.

## UNDP is one of the world's largest brokers of climate change adaptation and mitigation country projects, with a current portfolio of USD 1.34 billion in mitigation and adaptation grant-financed projects in over 140 countries, supported by co-funding of $67 billion. The work builds on UNDP's expertise and experience in addressing the root causes of poverty through a range of interventions that generally amounts to approximately US$750 million. UNDP's expertise ranges from strengthening forest governance and improving land tenure security (to increase access to resources, reduce conflict and strengthen accountability for decision-making); developing financial systems and mechanisms to underpin REDD+ implementation; building the capacity of governments and communities in delivery systems, national REDD+ related PAMs; working with the private sector and governments to promote deforestation-free commodity supply chains, ensuring the inclusion of indigenous peoples and other local communities in benefit sharing arrangements; supporting the development of independent communities, to ensure they are more sustainable and inclusive; and strengthening the management of protected areas, including indigenous and community conservation areas.

The Unit was created in 2007 and was duly upgraded to a 'Directorate' on April 4, 2018.

137\. The Fund also builds on UNDP’s experience as a partner of the UN-REDD Programme, a delivery partner of the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (FCPF), as well as an implementing partner for the Governors Climate and Forests Taskforce Initiative, and CAFI. Within the UN-REDD Programme, UNDP has been involved in supporting countries to develop their National Communication, and has been instrumental in supporting countries to develop national safeguard systems and grievance mechanisms. As a delivery partner of FCPF, UNDP is also an implementing partner for the Forest Productivity Improvement Component (FPIC) to REDD+. UNDP plays a leading role in supporting countries to develop national REDD+ strategies and action plans that are inclusive and which respond to national development priorities as well as international commitments.

138\. UNDP already has experience in supporting a number of countries to transition into Phase II REDD+ implementation. For example, in Viet Nam, UNDP is the lead facilitating agency for the Norway-funded project “Supporting Vietnam’s National Forest Strategy and Action Plan (NFSAP)”. This position for the implementation of the USD 30 million first phase of Norway’s $1 billion bilateral REDD+ agreement. In the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC), UNDP has led the establishment of the National REDD+ Task Force, which is responsible for coordinating and implementing REDD+ projects and managing over $60 million under CAFI. In Ecuador, UNDP led the development of the first-ever REDD+ project in the country, which was implemented by the Ministry of Environment (MMA) with UNDP’s support.

139\. UNDP has a team of 35 REDD+ experts supporting more than 40 countries. They are based in regional offices and in New York and Geneva. The team has excellent working relations with key REDD+ donors, such as Norway, the UK, Germany, Denmark, and Switzerland, and recognizes its ability to provide high-quality, trusted technical advice to many of the most rapid advancing REDD+ countries around the world. UNDP also has a team of 11 experts in the Green Commodities Programme, which focuses on the development of market-based mechanisms that can provide technical support to the UNDP Country Office in Ghana throughout project implementation.

140\. The UNDP Ghana Country Office was established in 1978. As a long-term and trusted government partner, UNDP is recognized by national and local stakeholders for playing a relevant role in the country’s development agenda. Since its establishment, UNDP has been working with the Government of Ghana to mainstream climate change into sectoral policies and plans through policy frameworks in the country. UNDP has also supported Government in accessing vertical funds such as the GEF and the Global Environment Facility (GEF). Since 2013, UNDP has co-chairs the Environmental Sector Working Group (ENSWG) on behalf of developing partners. The ENSWG was established in adherence to the principles of Aid Effectiveness. The Country Office has also been working with the Ghanaian government and other stakeholders through initiatives which complements its other initiatives in the environment portfolio under the United Nations Sustainable Development Partnership and the UNDP Country Programme Document, as described in 5.E.1.

141\. Global Shea Alliance (GSA) is a non-profit industry association based in Accra with 500 members from across Africa and Europe. The GSA was established to promote sustainable production through public-private partnerships, the GSA promotes industry sustainability, quality practices and standards for shea butter and its derivatives. The GSA has signed two multi-year agreements: The Sustainable Shea Initiative, which is a USD 13 million, five-year public-partnership agreement to promote shea markets worldwide and improve sustainable production in Africa signed between the GSA and USAID.
