**142. During project implementation, various key partners will participate. These roles will be further confirmed and determined. These are including but not limited to the following government agencies, research institutions, CCOs and networks:**

- **Ministry of Food and Agriculture (MOFA):** Its mission is to promote sustainable agriculture and thrive in the sector through policy formulation, implementation, monitoring and evaluation, and other support services to farmers. It will have an important role (in particular its decentralized extension division) in implementing the project activities at the district level and ensuring alignment with project goals. MOFA is fully decentralized with 7 directorates as well as information and monitoring officers at Regional and District levels. The majority of the staff working on agricultural extension services come under the Local Government Services. The research institute under MOFA is the Centre for Scientific and Technological Research Institute of Ghana (CSTIRG).

- **Forestry Research Institute of Ghana (FORIG):** This is a research institute under the Council for Scientific and Industrial Research (CSIR) that provides scientific information on forest resources, social, economic and environmental benefits of society. The project will rely on its expertise for applied research support.

- **Environmental Protection Agency (EPA)**: The National Focal Point for Climate Change and is responsible for all National Communication to the UNFCCC. It also hosts Ghana Climate Change Data Hub (GCCDH) which provides a platform for climate change data sharing and dissemination including a Domestic Electronic Registry System for climate actions in ministries, cities and project levels.

- **Ghana Cocoa Board (GCB):** The GCB is responsible for the promotion of cocoa production, processing and marketing of good quality cocoa, coffee and shea fruit in all forms in the most efficient manner. It will be involved in the implementation of the project activities at the District Extension Division (CHED), which currently has little or no agents responsible for the shea sector in the region. The GCB will collaborate with the Ghana Cocoa Board Training Centre (GCBTC) and the CCOs sub-station in Bole. The project will collaborate with GCR on training for the on-farm trials and propagation of improved varieties. It will also draft a manual which will be finalized during the life of the project. It will also develop the framework to implement the Shea Manual.

- **Kwame Nkrumah University of Science and Technology (KNUST):** Its mission is to provide an environment for research, teaching and learning in science and technology that contributes towards the industrial and socio-economic development of Ghana, Africa and other nations. The project will collaborate with KNUST to develop a manual on best practices in shea production and knowledge management aspects of the project.

- **A Research Centre for Agriculture Industry Centre, Noi, Birdlife International, IUCN Ghana and ClientEarth/Taylor Crabb Initiative:** These are well established NGOs in the country and the NSZ with substantial experience in CSF, CBFM, development and management, community-based conservation and forest land tenure issues.

- **Northern Development Authority (NDA):** It was set up by an Act of Parliament, Act 963, 2017 to provide a one-stop-shop for all matters relating to the implementation of the Northern Development Zone (NDZ) and for related matters. The NDA has regional offices in the three northern regions.

- **Shea Network Ghana:** It is a multi-stakeholder network whose mission is to engender a common national shea agenda, facilitate and coordinate the efforts of all stakeholders in promoting shea. It will be fully on work at the multi-stakeholder engagement.

B.5. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

143. The proposal makes a case for a grant (100% co-financing), on the basis of the following inter-related elements: (i) all the project components target barriers which, in principle, need to be addressed before private sector actors can be engaged; and (ii) there are no other sources of public finances (e.g., limitations to government taking on concession debt in the current challenging macroeconomic circumstances), such public expenditures can now only come from international grants.

144. The GCF grant request is strategic, adds value and ensures the viability of various benefit streams and will be applied to all four outputs. First, the constituencies that the project addresses are the beneficiaries of climate change and therefore need support in order to adapt to it. Second, the areas being impacted by climate change. Funding will help them to meet emission reduction objectives while also addressing other barriers.

145. Second: Output 2 which attracts private sector on-financing is not currently viable on its own in the land use sector, as these are unable to tackle the systemic issues in the value chain that are at the root of their inability to provide a stable supply of quality kermes. The kermes value chain depends on women to collect the kermes but supply is diminishing. If women, organised in cooperatives would be guaranteed of fair prices and market, various agents (small enterprises, larger companies) will be able to invest in the sector and ensure that they have access to high quality kermes (due to better storage) and ensure the supply of shea thus stabilizing the value chain in the long term. The project will address this by providing a stable supply of kermes through private sectors, whereby performance contracts are set up with groups of women who plant and collect shea in the project area. This will also provide an income for these women to ensure their survival rates in the early stages of tree growth. Tree planting activities will be implemented through the new partnership between USAID and the Government of Ghana (Ghana Shea Alliance) and Intercluster. Global Shea Alliance and partners will embed these performance indicators to ensure survival rates are met. The project will also provide a stable supply of shea for private sector buyers to contribute to tree monitoring and link free production with incomes. All parties will be responsible for the tree survival, not just the farmers. Together, they will define which monitoring and support activities need to take place.

146. The proposed project is the first attempt to plant Shea trees at such a large scale, and thus it will have a positive impact in terms of cost reduction, development of proven methods, as well as demonstrating that this can be done on a large scale with minimal disruption to existing livelihoods in the absence of additional grant funding. First, the nursery infrastructure will stay with the communities, which are already involved in Shea production and thus will not require any new investments for nurseries, parkland management. Second, because of its scale, the project will remove the current barriers or negative incentives (e.g., lack of access to credit, lack of market information, lack of extension services etc.) so that it is easier for private companies to replicate this effort in other parts of their supply chain. The USA has seen that previous PFPRs it has implemented have used to new projects by private sector actors. This project will provide a model and the opportunity to create necessary partnerships to do so.

147. Third, the fiscal space is severely constrained and there are challenges taking on concessional debt. Ghana is currently under a 3-year extended credit arrangement (EGA; 2015-2018), extended for 1 year with closure requirements in 2019) with the IMF, the objectives of which

## B

### 148.
Local private sector investment in Ghana has been hindered by the very high cost of credit (the policy rate for government has now come down to 18% from 25% in 2016), but commercial rates are still at 27%. The project will be able to access concessional financing through the GCF. Moreover, access to finance is much more limited in the Northern Savannah where the risk premium is perceived to be effectively higher. Activities that can reduce the risk-premium for business and investors would be very welcome. However, the project is not investing in any activities that would 'crowd out' the private sector as they are not expected to generate significant returns on their own. The evidence of commitment of private sector co-financing and parallel financing demonstrates how financial institutions can be encouraged to take a side of show processes once unconditional investments can be undertaken on the supply side.

### 149.
The GCF grant will pump finance activities directly linked to climate change mitigation and adaptation. The detailed models and financial analysis are presented in Annex 3.

### 150.
The grant financing leverages about USD 24 million in co-financing from private sector and government sources, which is a significant increase compared with the previous project. This would not happen but for the strategic investments through the GCF. It also leverages close to 50% of the available resources from the GCF.

## B.6 Exit strategy and sustainability (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

### 151.
Output 1 will increase the resilience of a population in an area covering 440,000 hectares of land and forests through improved livelihoods (Activity 1.2 & 3), development of forests (Activity 1.1, 1.2 & 1.5), the restoration of ecosystem services (Activity 1.2 & 1.4), reduction of risks from fire (Activities 1.1, 1.2 and 1.6) and improved land use planning (Activities 1.3 & 1.5). These activities will continue beyond the project period and the long term as capacities are built up in the communities. The project will also provide a strong basis for the scaling-up of proof-of-concept become evident through the stream of benefits. Equitable sharing of benefits will be developed by ensuring that all relevant groups and stakeholders, including women, youth, private companies and other bodies such as District Land Boards and Agriculture) will continue to support these processes and follow the necessary bureaucratic arrangement such as devolution of rights.

### 152.
The goal is transition the CREMA into self-sustaining community management entities will be based on the various streams of benefits that will be made possible by the project support. Revenues will be generated from the sale of timber, non-timber forest products and other activities. When rights are effectively devolved, and governance is clear, the community as a whole is recognized as the rights holder and the CREMA are enabled and empowered to govern the commons.

## CREAMA can retain the benefits through this social innovation which reconfigures social practices and norms.

The overall financial analysis of Output 2 shows estimated annual benefits over a 20 year period in the CREAMA follow-up scenario (CREMA). However, some highlights are:

- Benefits from woodfuel start accruing from year two with actual net benefits for the CREMA. Though largely fluctuating from Cedis 67,000 to 229,500 during the project period, these positive net benefits build up over time and reach a peak of Cedis 183,000 in year 14.
- The project will cover start-up and running costs during the project period, meaning that the net benefits for the CREMA will actually range from Cedis 721,000 to 838,000 during the project period, which is a significant amount. This means that the community will be able to continue using the CREMA with an ability to develop their activities further as well as resist unexpected shocks. It should also be noted that this scenario assumes that the women's cooperatives are able to sell woodcutters and processors, which represents therefore money benefiting the community.

**Trends on shea:** Globally, the shea export market is in a competitive industry mostly due to better bargaining/imports from manufacturers/chocolatiers. The global growth of chocolate consumption is increasing the demand for cocoa-based ingredients utilized in the production of chocolates; for specific brands, the demand for cocoa butter has increased by 10% over the past five years. Cocoa butter is used as a base ingredient in chocolate spreads, chocolate and chocolate things/cups, coatings, extrusion, and moulding. The number of confectionary product lines using shea butter has also increased due to its high melting point and ability to withstand high temperatures. Shea butter is also used for the production of cosmetics such as lipsticks, moisturizers, shampoos, conditioners, hair oils, and skin care products. Shea butter is also used in the food industry for the following reasons: it is a growing oil demand which had a 6.4% CAGR since 2015; it demonstrates preference for palm oil products; it is healthy and natural ingredient—shea-marks do not increase LDL cholesterol levels; and it has a high melting point and ability to withstand high temperatures. The global market size of shea butter was valued at $38 million in 2019, with the United States being number one in the US, France, United Kingdom, Japan, and Germany and number two in Brazil, India, and China (World Data – average 2014-2015).

The project will help transform Ghana's Shea commodity sector to become more climate-smart using less wood in village-scale processing, which in turn reduces pressure on forest resources. The project will also provide women with new and improved tools for shea harvesting, creating more profitable business opportunities for rural women. 370 women will be trained in shea restoration and expansion, including the use of agroforestry techniques to improve soil fertility and water retention. Shea-related infrastructures and 70 village nurseries, employing 700 women, will support the supply of seedlings for new plantations. The project will also provide training on business management skills that will add value in local communities and incentivize long-term participation in landscape management.

This integrated system across the value chain, with increasing upstream incomes early in the project will create incentives for shea restoration and sustainable management in the long term. Investment by private sector companies and NGOs coming together to implement project activities in beneficiary communities through a multi-stakeholder approach will ensure that the benefits of increased production of shea and other trees and have undertaken business, management and shea production trainings to ensure the women cooperatives are able to sustain themselves after the project phases out.

With Taungya Groups, the estimated annual benefit for farmers is expected to be between 0 and 8 from intercropping from year 2-5.5, in a range from Cedis 154 to Cedis 675 per farmahaa. A conservative approach has been taken and benefits from only one or two crops are considered with production only projected for the first five years.

Other highlights include:

* Farmers will not see any benefits but a large benefit of 9,883 is expected in B from sawn timber, as well as 771 in years 16 and 20
* Profit from Taungya Groups will go to the MTS farmers for their labour in setting-up the system (nursery + plantation)
* As for CRBMS, most of all costs will go to wood cutters, which is money remaining in the community.

## 159. The approach to restore profitability and ecosystem services within the forest reserves will be proven.

The Forestry Commission can then approve the application of other forest reserves for similar activities, and the project will be based on a sale of timber from these areas in managed plantation zones in the reserves. Benefits to the community include the flexibility to own all food crops produced by the project, as well as the ability to sell them at market prices. The project will also go beyond subsistence agriculture and farmers can earn in surpluses. The project will have therefore laid the foundation for a sustainable forest management system that will provide new market options storage and transport facilities to channel the food produced to the market. For example, the "One District, One Warehouse" being undertaken by the Ministry of Food and Agriculture (MoFA) is an initiative that could be expanded in the future to include other agricultural sidelines ventures that will see beneficiaries expanding return on investments to more inhabitants in the project area.

## 160. At the national level: The Warsaw Framework requirements are fulfilled as a result of this grant funding from the GCF, especially with respect to safeguarded requirements. Ghana will assess the eligibility to receive results based payments from the NSZ and other programmes by tapping into opportunities for private sector investment in forest restoration and sustainable land use, and the mechanism established through Article 6 of the Paris Agreement. It is exploring the opportunity to create a national carbon fund that would attract public-private financing (including the recent ERPA signed with the World Bank's Carbon Fund) to develop a sustainability platform which leverages and enables re-investment in the forests and landscapes sector.

## 161. The project will demonstrate that tree planting can be achieved at scale for Shea. The model for restoration of degraded land through tree planting will be based on the creation and strengthening of established partnerships (NGOs, communities) and infrastructure (nurseries, warehouses). In this way, the project will create a long-term relationship with the community and ensure the sustainability of shea over the long term at the district and regional levels is expected as the demand for quality shea continues to grow for many years to come. The project will create a long-term relationship

## Adaptation impact

### The project has significant benefits to enable increases in ecosystem services (through restoration of natural resources) and the provision of food security. These benefits will be provided by restored natural resources positively affecting 100,00 direct beneficiaries and 540,200 indirect beneficiaries.

The project is also expected to have a positive impact on poverty reduction. The World Bank has noted for the NZS noting that the latest (2018) China Living Standards Survey indicates continuing high poverty rates in the Northern, Upper West and Upper East regions, with the NZS the only ecological region in the country where forest cover is increasing.

Furthermore, the project helps to bring about resilience to environmental shocks such as droughts and floods. This will be achieved through the establishment and planting of trees throughout the landscape. It secures the supply of energy sources – reducing the need to access and degrade forest areas for wood fuel. The project also provides a range of other benefits including improved soil fertility, increased yields in food crops (such as yam) which have high nutritional value), reduced vulnerability of communities and provide options and a safety net should subsistence cropping systems fail. Forests that will be set up in the project area are expected to provide additional income opportunities for local communities through the production of non-timber forest products including beverages, dyes, fibres and many other products. It is anticipated that benefits will accrue to those that are already engaged in these activities as well as new entrants into this sector.

### More broadly, by increasing areas of forest across the NZS, strengthening their management, increasing the protection of river catchments and increasing the number of trees on farm, the project will have important impacts on soil fertility, localized climate and management of the water cycle. These improvements in these areas are expected to lead to increased agricultural productivity.

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 53 OF 83

#### D

**D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)**

The Forest Commission has identified specific targets in the National Biodiversity Strategy and the Forest Sector Strategic Plan (FSSP) to increase forest cover from its current low of 4% to high forest zone due to capacity and costs. By setting up the project in the NSZ, capacity would be strengthened in the REDD+ strategy. The project will also provide a platform for scaling up community-based enterprises to the communities in the NSZ through the CREMA and directly for the MTS. The paradigm shift will come about from enabling communities to generate revenue from sustainable forest management activities that are compatible with conservation of biodiversity, carbon sequestration and ecosystem services. They can do this whilst generating emission reductions which they will be eligible for future payments. In the meantime, they will also benefit from increased income from community-based enterprises such as those from community forest management and shea collection, processing and sale that will be available for further development for community needs, which will continue into the future.

CREMA and women's cooperatives are equipped with business planning skills, financial capacity and access to markets. They can now use this knowledge to diversify their income (greenhouse, stocks of products, saw machines, woodlots). A planned fund at the national level managing use of proceeds from REDD+ will act as an additional source for continuing climate change mitigation and adaptation activities in the NSZ in Ghana as a whole.

The project places strong emphasis on the fundamental role that communities and especially women can play in the sustainable management of forest resources, once capacity, resources, governance structures are in place. The project will support the establishment of Management committees and contracts with Taungu communities and FC). Building on lessons learnt from previous experiences in the country and the region, the project will support the government in undertaking a review of existing legislation to ensure that it is consistent with the new Forest Bill before Parliament and to create enabling conditions for scaling up, in forest and tree tenure and benefit sharing. The project will also support the development of a legal framework for the transfer of rights associated with community management of the CREMA - tree tenure - to include ownership and disposal. As noted, the difference in this CREMA model which goes beyond wildlife management is that it includes trees and other forest resources.

Planting of shea trees in the woodlands of the NSZ increases the potential availability of shea kernels for more than 50 years to come, and thereby has a transformational impact for the value chain. The project will plant 17.5 million shea seedlings and 450,000 other high value species in the agroforestry partnership (these figures already take into account mortality rates). Most of the

* Regions in NSZ that still have sufficient closed forest cover, such as the area around the Ta National Park, enjoy a more or less stable rainfall due to the micro-climate created by the Park

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 53 OF 83

#### 173.
*Women are already aware of the future benefits of shea and as aggregation and marketing of current shea stocks increase the value and income (through direct marketing) of shea for the women, they will be more willing to plant new trees. This will reduce their need for firewood (heating and watering). Increasing the number of easily accessible shea trees will increase the quantity of shea nuts produced by each tree and thus increase the income from them. These are all available sources of shea supply for food and cosmetic products. The increased availability of shea nuts, fruits and the revival of these trees in providing fruits, shade, wood fuel, butter and tea for medicines at the household level will also provide additional sources of income. The production of shea oil and by-products such as soap and wood fuel provide important additional sources of income. The expected annual revenue from the sale of shea nuts (at a price of $10 per kg) and shea oil (at a price of $25 per kg) and wood-related cost savings for 20,000 women collectors is estimated at $3,150,000.* This will be accompanied by a process which ensures that women are able to continue playing a major role in the shea tree production. The project will also provide training on business management skills and their empowerment and putting in place the conditions for increased decision-making for 20,000 women.

#### 174.
*Embedded in this project are various outputs addressing drivers of forest degradation that provide co-benefit adaptation co-benefits for target populations and communities, especially women and poor households. Restoration activities ensure the provision of buffering ecosystem services that will reduce the risk of fire occurrence. The project will also implement a number of management measures to prevent the occurrence of fires, exacerbated by temperature increase and drier conditions in degraded landscapes.*

#### 175.
*The public private partnership model brings in substantial financial resources leveraged by GCF funds. This includes USD 20 million from contributing USD 30 million and will invest or are investing USD 50 million in downstream industrial facilities. Additionally, the project will leverage the deep experience of the private sector partners to ensure that the shea nuts are processed into high quality products. Each activity will be implemented by at least one company and an NGO ensuring good quality of services and a strong commitment to sustainability. The project will also include measures to ensure that these activities continue in the long term. Funding from the private sector partners will result in private sector companies developing new shea-based ingredients, improving their operating efficiency, and access greater quality kernels. Such enhanced capacity will also add value-in-currency to the additional 50,000 kg of shea kernels expected to be sold annually by the project. This will provide a strong foundation for the shea value chain for long term sustainability of the shea sector. The high profile, visibility, and contribution to the Green Climate Fund (GCF) are also important co-benefits. The project is aligned with the goal of tree restoration and economic growth is well understood at the country level, promoting long-term investments.*

#### 176.
*This project supports the development of a NSB national FRL, with field specific data to enable the refinement of the national FRL. It also allows for further training of safeguards focal points as well as communication and knowledge management of achievements and lessons learnt during GCF/FR implementation.*

**Page 58**
---

177. The GESLERP is the first REDD+ project being developed in a savannah zone which makes it a flagship project and enables the scale up of Ghana's evolution to national REDD+ implementation and emissions reductions from the land and forest sector.

Potential for scaling up and replication

178. Next, there are several Forest Reserves (FRs) in NSZ and in other parts of the country once it is demonstrated that revenues from timber and non-timber products can indeed sustain the CREMA operations. As has been noted, the unique feature of the project's approach is a focus on active, profitable management practices which have the potential to be scaled up. The project has identified what appears to be a financially viable forest based green "enterprise". New communities can adopt these models as best practice and replicate them in their own areas with appropriate training and implementation modalities. These communities can receive some start-up funds from the Government through the Municipal and District assemblies as proof of concept will have been obtained.

179. In this way, the project will be able to greatly scale up community management of forest resources in NSZ and other parts of Ghana. The project has already demonstrated that communities are further convinced of the importance of the community management model, to enable a wider set of benefits including ecosystem service benefits as CREMA are zoned to include protection areas. The key is to ensure that communities are able to continue to benefit from these practices (and not to continuously seek grants but to get to a level of national consciousness where all these practices are integrated into the fabric of their lives and become part of a way of life). This sets the stage for large scale transitions and changes of the landscape dynamics.

180. The project will be scalable or replicable in the absence of additional grant funding. First, the nursery infrastructure will stay with the communities, who will be able to amplify the restoration efforts through their own activities. Second, the communities have already demonstrated their willingness to continue Shea collection and maintain revenues from wood and other non-wood products. Second, because of the high cost of planting trees (high seedling costs, lack of knowledge, lack of technical skills) it is easier for private companies to replicate this effort in other parts of their supply chain. The GSA has seen that private companies are already interested in replicating this model and have been willing to pay for it if they have been able to evaluate the benefits and draw on the necessary partnerships to do so. Similarly, women's groups have also demonstrated their willingness to scale up operations as there will be a continuing and growing demand for Shea kernels.

181. Global demand for Shea has increased by 600% over the last 20 years, and this trend is expected to continue, with a further 50% increase forecasted by industry players for the next 5 years. With the project's approach, there will be an opportunity to scale up the production of Shea kernels in the presence of Shea kernels exported that are exported every year are used as ingredient in confectionary, bakery and cosmetic products. Five companies will be improving and increasing the facilities to enable them to process Shea kernels into a range of food ingredients.

182. The MTS approach can be scaled up to other Forest Reserves in the NSZ and other parts of the country (280 forest reserves) and in West Africa.

183. The project also has a high potential to create an enabling environment for knowledge generation. This is due to the nature of project interventions, which include applied research capacity building activities across some activities and stakeholder engagement and networking. ### D.4. Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### 184. Development of the Northern Savannah Ecological Zone is an inspiration for Ghana's economic growth and poverty alleviation. The zone has high potential in terms of food security, employment generation, and poverty alleviation. Poverty rates are almost twice as high as the national rate. Agriculture land is becoming increasingly degraded due to overgrazing and deforestation. With the proposed investments, targeted investments could expand choices for revenue generation, lower food prices and improve food security, increase growth and employment, and reduce poverty throughout Ghana. Thus, the slogan "Agriculture is the foundation of development" will be realized in this zone. In addition, increased incomes from forests for communities will benefit community districts and will help to reverse deforestation. The project will also contribute to the country's climate change adaptation goals as the value share chain will be beneficial to the country as a whole, increasing trade and economic benefits and promoting sustainable development.

#### 185. Community management of forests can be tested across scales and the institutional frameworks for natural resource management can be strengthened to pave way for land use planning and balanced forest management in the zone. The project will also create incentives for communities to shift production systems from a major source of livelihoods for shea-growing communities but currently more marginal place of the farmer at the local village level is low. The project will create incentives to complement farmers' income through value chain development, including the creation of a Shea Value Chain, from restoration and sustainable supply to increasing the interest of buyers and the integration of smallholder farmers into the market.

#### 186. In order to protect vulnerable groups, at the implementation stage, the GSA will launch a call for public-private partnerships. Each public-private partnership will consist of one private sector company (e.g., agro-processing companies) that will be responsible for providing services such as KPIs, location, budget, and experience. Each partnership will be assessed by a technical review committee to ensure that the service provider has the capacity to deliver on its commitments under this payment agreement with the NGO, which will include both GCF-funded and private sector funded results. The public-private partnerships will be required to provide a financial plan to ensure that communities start receiving economic benefits from shea while the trees are growing. The private sector will not contribute grants; rather they spend their money to implement activities directly related to the project. These activities may include, but are not limited to, the following: activities: warehouse construction, equipment, trainings related to cooperative management, business development services, and other activities that will be determined by the GSA. Women's cooperatives have the option to sell their shea to the private sector company with whom they are working or to other companies in the market. The private sector company must buy at the best conditions; thus ensuring there is no price fixing that could marginalize them further.

#### 187. Access to finance is limited and attracting skilled labour and managerial capabilities into the NSZ is difficult due to poor infrastructure, lack of access to markets, and low levels of education. The project will address some of these issues by incentivizing and crowding-in private sector investment amongst others. An enabling mechanism for managing social and environmental risks can help to address the sustainability challenges in the NSZ. This includes land tenure security and other institutional arrangements which must be done in a manner which addresses clarification of land ownership and use where this is necessary.

#### 188. Ghana's third national communication to the UNFCCC provides a comprehensive picture of financial, technical, capacity and institutional needs and gaps. These include all aspects of data processing and management, training and conducting monitoring, review and verification of GHG

## D.5. Country ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

In the past few years, Ghana has made significant progress in mainstreaming climate change into national development planning and policy by establishing a solid policy framework and institutional structure to tackle climate change. Agriculture, forestry and other land use (AFOLU) is the largest contributor of GHG emissions in Ghana, accounting for 70% of total GHG emissions in the Third National Communication. The project is coherent with the following existing plans and policies:

- **National Climate Change Policy**, in particular Focus Area 4 (increase carbon sinks) which aims to minimize the loss of carbon sinks by reducing activities that lead to the destruction of natural ecosystems, such as deforestation, forest degradation, and conversion of forests into croplands through programmes that restore degraded forests and ecosystems.
- **National REDD+ Strategy**: The National REDD+ Strategy is responsive to the Policy, with several programmes to increase carbon sinks through improving governance (4.1), strengthening the integration of climate change adaptation in land use planning and management (4.2), conservation of trees through agroforestry (4.5), community based natural resource management (3.2). All these areas strongly feature in the various components of the project.
- **National Development Plan 2017–2021**: The National Development Plan 2017–2021 and Policy on Action “promoting sustainable utilization of forest resources through REDD+” in the NDC, and its related Programme for the Sustainable Management of Forest Resources (PMSMR) and the “National REDD+ Strategy” on a national basis,” and “wildlife management in the transboundary and savannah dry lands in Ghana.” Investment in these areas is also aligned with the project’s focus area on increasing carbon sinks.
- **National REDD+ Strategy**: The strategy to implement REDD+ at a national scale, but to roll out concerted actions and activities at sub-national landscape scales. One of two sub-national pilot landscapes is the Sheba Landscape (The Sheba Landscape Programme). The Sheba Landscape Programme for the Sheba Landscape of the Northern Savannah Woodland (The Sheba Savannah Woodland Programme): The GSLEHP contributes three elements of the REDD+ Strategy to be implemented in the landscape: a) Transform the landscape into a carbon sink through sustainable forest management and forest restoration; b) Transform the landscape into an area with high biodiversity, including wildlife, by promoting agroforestry, smart production systems and landscapes; and c) Expand platforms for cross-sector and public-private collaboration on climate change adaptation and mitigation in line with the National REDD+ Strategy.
- **The National Climate Change Adaptation Strategy** insists for a consistent, comprehensive and a targeted approach to increasing climate resilience and decrease vulnerability of the population. The project will also provide data for the GCF, which now requires deployment and data to populate the system. GCF funding has also financed initial capacity building of safeguard focal points in the country.
- **Challenges**: The project is seeking funding for a NAMA to improve the sustainability of the carbon value chain. In this regard, the project will support community enterprise development for forest products.

The revised Forest and Wildlife Policy (2012) is my overall sector policy for conservation and sustainable development of forest and wildlife resources in Ghana. A Wildlife Resources

# GHANA FOREST DEVELOPMENT MASTER PLAN (2016-2036)

The vision of the FDMP is to achieve a just and equitable, and sustainable forest sector that promotes welfare and provides adequate means of livelihoods to all Ghanaians, in tandem with the 2012 Forest Policy.

## GHANA FOREST PLANTATION TARGET (2016-2040)

Seeks to achieve sustainable supply of planted trees and woodlots for a range of economic, social and environmental benefits through five strategic objectives:

* Provide a sustainable supply of timber and non-timber forest products, and environmental services.
* Achieve a 50% increase in the area under tree cover (from 1.2 million hectares to over 18 million hectares) by 2040.
* Increase the number of trees on farms to 37.5 million hectares of agricultural land.
* Conserve and enhance biodiversity through the application of best practice principles.
* Promote participation in the establishment and management of small, medium and large-scale forest plantations.

### Project Objectives

* Create employment opportunities and sustainable livelihoods in rural communities through forest plantations.
* Increase investments in research and development, extension, training and capacity building for forest plantations.
* Improve governance in the regulation and management of forest plantations.

## UN SUSTAINABLE DEVELOPMENT GOALS (SDGs)

The project is fully aligned to the United Nations Sustainable Development Partnership (UNSDP) 2016-2030 Agenda, which sets out the global goals for sustainable development initiatives and sets out the UN system's collective contributions to help the government and other stakeholders achieve these goals. The SDG framework recognizes that effective environmental governance at national and local levels is effective, efficient and coherent; and its Output 5.1 (national and sub-national institutions have greater capacity to implement environment and climate-related policies) is directly relevant to this project.

## GHANA REDD+ STRATEGY

The initial project idea stems from the Ghana REDD+ Strategy which builds on several rounds of consultations with stakeholders under the REDD+ Readiness Project. On 21 November 2016, the FC officially requested UNDP to support the development of a GCF project proposal to implement the Shea Landscape Programme of the REDD+ Strategy. UNDP commissioned an initial scoping study and subsequently established the NDA Technical Advisory Committee (TAC). The NDA nominated UNDP as AE for this project on 28 December 2016.

192. Specifically, a concept note was developed by the FC with support from UNDP with reviews from a multi-stakeholder National REDD+ Working Group constituted by the FC during the preparation of the project proposal. The concept note was reviewed and approved in June 2017 by the Board of Directors of the Manager for Evaluation, Monitoring and Budgeting; FC Northern Regional Manager for Forest Services Division; Wildfire Division, FC Head of Unit, COCOBOD; Ministry of Finance NDA; the head of the National REDD+ Working Group; and representatives from the Forestry Commission, National NGO to provide technical inputs and advisory support at each stage of the development of the project proposal. The concept note is available on the FC website for download by interested stakeholders in the sector and in the target area. Please see Annex 7 for a summary of the consultative process.

193. Under the leadership of FC's CEO, the FC organized a series of high-level meetings to mobilize and engage key stakeholders in the development of the project proposal. These meetings were held at Lands and Natural Resource and Members of Parliament from the NDA on 22 November 2017, and a meeting with the Minister for Environment, Science, Technology and Innovation (MESTI) was also presented to a wide range of stakeholders and high-level dignitaries during the 2nd National REDD+ Forum on 19 October 2017. The President of the Republic of Ghana, H.E. Nana Addo Dankwa Akufo-Addo, has committed to supporting the implementation of the project proposal as part of the national REDD+ programme in the Upper West, Upper East and Northern Regions of Ghana, with the support of the FC. The project proposal is currently being reviewed by the MESTI for submission to the Green Climate Fund. The programme has the potential to transform the northern savannah ecosystem of Ghana with the right implementation and enforcement structures.

**Green Climate Fund Readiness Programme**

194. The Ministry of Finance represented the Real Sector Division of Ministry of Finance to be the National Designated Authority (NDA) to liaise with the GCF. The Green Climate Fund (GCF) Readiness Programme, a joint partnership between UNDP, UNEP, and World Resource Institute (WRI), is being implemented in Ghana as part of the country's efforts towards accessing climate finance. The Programme in Ghana aimed to support the government in strengthening their national capacity for effective implementation of GCF projects and programmes, including climate financing in particular through the GCF. A GCF country programme is being developed through this readiness process.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 60 OF 83

## Cost-effectiveness

The project has explored the cost-effective options to implement the proposed activities, building on existing approaches to enable beneficiaries to establish and maintain their forests.

Cost-efficient approaches such as early burning as part of fire control, using community structures, creating nurseries for tree planting, and other low-cost techniques will be used. These are expected to yield a significant return in terms of forest establishment and maintenance over the first year of the project in areas that have established regeneration. Areas that have already gone through a participatory process with local communities, including women's groups, youth groups, and civil society organisations working already in the Shea sector will be project partners.

The MTS used in Output 3 is easily the most cost-efficient technique for plantation establishment and will deliver significant benefits to the environment and the economy. The project will also ensure that the beneficiaries incur the initial establishment costs such as ploughing/ weeding. Despite the wide project area, the total costs of establishing trees are expected to be strictly limited to 5% of the total costs thus limiting the chance for unnecessary operational costs.

### Effectiveness

The project is expected to be highly effective in delivering the proposed results because many of the enabling conditions are in place, there is significant capacity and competence of the implementing units where the constraints are more financial. This cost of USD 1.2 per CO2eq of establishing trees is significantly lower than other social uses of carbon (e.g., US Environmental Protection Agency) and compares favourably with other land use mitigation projects (e.g., USD$53 to USD $6.25 per t CO2eq for other projects funded by the GCF).

- Large scale adoption of best practices and lessons learned.

E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### 198. Project-level monitoring and evaluation will be undertaken in compliance with the UNDP POPD and the UNDP Evaluation Policy.
The primary responsibility for day-to-day project monitoring and implementation rests with the Project Manager. The Project Manager will develop annual work plans to ensure that all activities are completed on time, within budget, and according to the approved M&E plan. The POPD Country Office will monitor any delays or difficulties during implementation, including the implementation of the M&E plan. If necessary, appropriate corrective measures can be adopted. The M&E plan will cover the following monitoring indicators:

#### Output 1:
- **Forest management plans for land use planning components**, core conservation areas and areas under silviculture management
- **Certificate of Devaluation issued in CREMA**
- Number of forest management plans developed
- Number of communities and people involved in fire management
- Number of energy-saving kilns constructed and in use

#### Output 2:
- Number of area trees planted
- Number of other species of trees planted
- Number of nurseries set up
- Number of nurseries set up and capacity of the nurseries noted (number of seedlings available)
- Number of women trained and using grafting skills, business and accounting skills, reporting and number of private partnerships/contract

#### Output 3:
- Number of hectares of plantations set up
- Number of Taungya plots contracted for the Forestry Commission
- Acreage of Crop production and types of crops planted in MTS forest reserves
- Number of hectares of trees planted
- Number of large-scale nurseries set up and seedlings produced
- Number of people conducting fire breaks and conducting patrols every three months.

#### Output 4:
- Provision and integration of climatic information related to forest management in the NSZ into national level NFMS and GIS.
- Regular monitoring on forest cover and forest land use change
- Safeguard data and monitoring of the Environmental and Social Management Framework, Gender Action Plan and other plans under the ESFM framework.
- Maps of CREMA areas at community level.

### 199. The Project Manager will also ensure that all project staff maintain a high level of transparency, responsibility and accountability in monitoring and reporting project results.


---
**Page 77**
---

194\. The UNDP Country Office will support the Project Manager as needed, including through annual supervision missions. The UNDP Country Office is responsible for complying with UNDP project-level M&E requirements as outlined in the UNDP PJP. Additionally, the UNDP Country Office will provide quality assurance and troubleshooting support to the project via the UNDP Regional Technical Advisor as needed. The project target groups and stakeholders including the UNDA Focal Point will be involved as much as possible.

195\. A project inception workshop will be held after the UNDP project document has been signed by all relevant parties to: a) reorient project stakeholders to the project strategy and discuss any changes in the project design; b) review the project team structure, including roles and responsibilities of the project team, including reporting and communication lines and conflict resolution mechanisms; c) review the project management plan (M&Plan), including the project implementation schedule, the M&Plan, and the annual work plan. The Project Manager will prepare a draft of the M&Plan and the first year annual work plan. The Project Manager will prepare the inception report no later than one month after the inception workshop. The final inception report will be cleared by the UNDP Country Office and the UNDP Regional Technical Advisor (RTA) and approved by the Project Board.

196\. A project implementation report will be prepared for each year of project implementation. The Project Manager, the UNDP Country Office, and the UNDP RTA will provide objective input to the annual Progress Report (PIR). The PIR will include a summary of progress against the project milestones in the project results framework are monitored annually well in advance of the PIR submission deadline and will be shared with the Project Board and other stakeholders. The UNDP Country Office will coordinate the preparation of the PIR, including the annual work plan for the next year. The findings from the previous year's PIR will be used to inform the preparation of the next PIR\. The final project PIR, along with the terminal evaluation report and corresponding management response, will serve as the final project reporting document.

197\. An independent mid-term review (MTR) and an independent terminal evaluation (TE) (no later than three months prior to the operational closure of the project), will be undertaken and the findings and recommendations will be reported in a written report. The MTR will provide guidance for enhanced implementation during the final half of the project's duration as regards the MTR\. The terms of reference for both the MTR and TE are available on the UNDP Evaluation Resource Centre. The MTR and TE report will be cleared by the UNDP Country Office and the UNDP RTA and approved by the Project Board. The final reports will be available in English.

198\. The final TE report will be cleared by the UNDP Country Office and the UNDP RTA and will be approved by the Project Board. UNDP Country Office will include the planned project terminal evaluation report in the annual progress report (PIR) to be submitted to the UNDP Evaluation Resource Centre in English and the management response to the public UNDP Evaluation Resource Centre (EIRC) (www\.undp\.org).

# F. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F.1 Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)

| Selected Risk Factor 1 |
| --- |

### Description
Duty bearers do not have the capacity to meet their obligations in the Project.

### Category | Probability | Impact
Technical/Operational | Medium | Low

### Mitigation Measure(s)
The capacity and resources to be able to plan, implement, adopt and monitor all four outputs are key and provide a foundation for this throughout the project activities for duty-bearers including the FC regional and district staff, extension workers, NGO trainers, and community managers through the community executive committee. The FC will ensure that these capacity building measures are implemented in all areas. These include extension services for the value shelf chain. FC's ability to plan with communities, monitor, train, assess and adhere to contractual arrangements. Appropriate opportunities for governance, gender, natural resource management and other related issues have been identified. Adequate resources and adequate budget have been made in the budget for core group management groups. Monitoring methods and mechanisms are described above.

Legal and policy provisions are mostly in place; however the enforcement and implementation of these will need to be supported through the project activities, contracts and agreements and the integrated monitoring framework. The FC has initiated efforts towards the development of a more sustainable charcoal and fuel production system<sup>16</sup>, and the project activities will implement this through collection, processing and sale of charcoal.

Mitigation and management measures will lower the probability of this risk occurring to 'low'.

<sub><sup>16</sup>The 2014 Forest Development Master Plan (FDMP) identifies a key programme as the Sustainable Production and Management of Commercial Wood Fuel in the Savannah Woodland (Programme 1.8)</sub>

Selected Risk Factor 2 and 3

| Description | Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| a) Threats to tree species biodiversity as shea is restored in the landscape<br>• Inadequate water for set-up of new nurseries and other water needs during shea processing. Seedlings will need to be watered daily, at a rate of 10-15 liters per every 200 seedlings during the dry season (when there may not be sufficient water available in parklands and forest reserves will need to be regularly watered)<br>b) Water Balance Estimates under Climate Change Scenarios; currently there is less water available on the landscape in the long term<br>c) Future precipitation levels are uncertain (see Table 10 for the project area).<br>• Pollution Prevention and Resource Efficiency: The Project potentially can result in the release of pollutants to the environment due to routine or non-routine activities, including potential pollution from pesticides and/or transboundary impacts.<br>d) The Project may involve the application of pesticides that may have adverse effects on the environment and human health. Pollutants can include fertilizers or herbicides used to manage forest plantations. Plastic bags for seedlings may also cause pollution | Social and environmental | Low (<5% of project value) | LowLow |
| Measure (a) | a) Shea trees are the main species to be restored in the parklands, however other species will also be planted such that overall biodiversity is not threatened (See para 91). These species will also help to improve pollination services on the landscape. Shea occurs in the wild with other species and does not dominate as does shea in plantations. Shea seedlings are a mixture of trees on the landscape in fellows, farmlands and within community forest areas.<br>b) Only seedlings in the nurseries will be watered. The project aims to leverage the “one village one dam” programme (see para 102). This is an initiative that encourages local communities to establish user associations with local ownership and control. Studies indicate sufficient supplies of ground water and rainfall are available for the establishment of shea seedlings in the parklands where women have established themselves and will be strong enough to survive after two years in nurseries. Thereafter the following measures will be taken by MTS farmers and women planting shea:<br>• mulching to keep the soil moist<br>• leaving a little gap in the planting hole to retain as much water as possible; and<br>• loosening the soil at the base of the planting hole to encourage deep rooting as much as possible |  |  |

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 76 OF 83

### c) Numerous studies provide a concrete water balance estimate to achieve the results of the project under climate change scenarios. These studies project that there will be sufficient ground water to recharge water bodies and enough water to continue irrigation of crops and trees.

*East Africa: Ethiopia, Kenya, Uganda, Rwanda, Burundi, Volta and Rio Basin; Emmanuel Bokoko, Frederick Yaw Logan, Barnabas Amisigo, and Mark Osei-Owuor.*

### d) Spraying of fungicides and herbicides will be avoided. Fertilizer recommendations for nursery seedling production and field establishment will be assessed by CRIG and KNUST experts. There will be minimal generation of waste. Charcoal making will be conducted so as not to affect communities (through smoke). The charcoal will be used in the cooking process. The composting of organic waste will be done through improved kims which have higher efficiency than traditional earth-mound kims. The efficiency of improved kims is approximately 30%-42% compared with traditional kims (15-20% water content) while the efficiency of the improved kims is approximately 30%-42%. As compared with traditional kims, it reduces emissions and can reduce emissions to the atmosphere by up to 76%.

### e) The use of pesticides will be minimal.

The main control methods will involve the use of resistant hybrids, weeding will be done manually, protecting young plants with collar wire and destroying nesting/breeding areas of pests will be considered as part of the management agreement. If chemical control is necessary, it will only be used if not available, the collection, re-use and proper disposal will be included in the management agreement with the FC.

### f) A regular pest and disease observation will be maintained to help prevent the outbreak and spread of any potential diseased/infested plantations.


| Selected Risk Factor 3 | Description | Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Risks related to private sector engagement and requirement to carry out due diligence for example to identify risks related to money laundering, terrorist financing, and prohibited practices? | Other | Low | High |

### Mitigation Measures

A Partner Capacity Assessment and a micro-assessment have been carried out for the Forestry Commission and Ghana Shrub Board. Risks such as money laundering, terrorist financing, and prohibited practices risk were identified under UNDP’s Policy on Anti-Corruption (the “UNDP Policy”) and the guidelines of UNDP for the private companies providing co-financing will be available. Under the terms of the FCP, UNDP has a commitment to ensure that all projects are implemented in accordance with the UNDP Policy. Control practices are fully identified and considered in the programme/project design and processes and that adequate measures have been put in place to mitigate these risks. The UNDP Policy is aligned to the processes and requirements set out in its capacity assessment tools for partnerships, carrying out a thorough due diligence process on all partners. In addition, the Ghana Shrub Board and Forestry Project UNDP applies a zero-tolerance policy in relation to fraud and corruption. The guiding principles of UNDP’s commitment to anti-corruption are contained within the UNDP Policy. The UNDP Policy is aligned with the UNDP Policy against Fraud and Corruption Practices (the “FCP Policy”), which applies to all activities and operations of UNDP, including its partnerships. The FCP Policy sets out a framework for implementing the UNDP Policy against Fraud and Corruption Practices in relation to procurement, financial management, control and accountability are vested. In relation to the activities and operations of UNDP, the implementation of the FCP Policy is aligned with the control practices was reaffirmed in the Accreditation Master Agreement signed between the GCF and UNDP on 5 August 2016.

### GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

#### G. Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

**193. UNDP's Social and Environmental Standards (SES) were reviewed by the GCF accreditation panel and deemed sufficient to accredit UNDP to low "and moderate" risks projects. The overall social and environmental risk assessment is conducted in accordance with the SES. This includes the environmental and social screening conducted for this project and its full environmental and social impact assessment (ESIA). The ESIA will be carried out by an independent consultant or by the project development team, a safeguards consultant who consulted with civil society**, community traditional leaders and the Global Shea Alliance and on the consultative process with the task force set up in Ghana.

**194. As a Moderate Risk Project, further impact assessment and management measures will be needed in order to manage risks effectively throughout project implementation. These steps and components are described in Annex 6: Environmental and Social Management Framework. The first step during project development is to identify key environmental and social issues that may arise as the project will be carried out by experts and will involve research, consultations, field work, stakeholder engagement and monitoring of project activities. This will be done through a participatory process with all actors clarified and finalized. The ESIA will cover the activities planned by the project including final selection of community-based organizations (CBOs) for Shea tree planting, establishment of Shea nurseries, MFS, confirmation of smallholder farms and bush fallow areas for Shea restoration, and setting up women's cooperatives, warehouses and processing facilities. Potential issues may arise whereby men could co-opt women in the process of Shea tree planting and harvesting. Other potential risks include conflicts with wildlife and biodiversity conservation such as selection of tree species, land use planning, rights pertaining to biodiversity conservation such as 197. Finally, the project’s approach is bolstered by the principles embedded in Ghana’s national REDD+ strategy and the accompanying national ESFM, which are to be respected in project planning and implementation as follows:
- Recognize and respect the rights of all people and segments of society, including landowners, land users, indigenous peoples, women and children.
- Maintain and enhance the integrity of Ghana’s forests and its environment in order to safeguard ecosystem services.
- Implement a process that is open, inclusive, equitable, and transparent process at all levels and at all times.
- No promotion of external interests or ‘white capture’ at the expense of people’, communities or the nation as a whole.
- Align with national development goals and aspirations.

198. Through the social and environmental screening procedure and consultations with women’s groups, traditional authorities, community-based organizations, civil society organizations at the district level, government institutions, private sector and a range of partners and relevant projects, a careful assessment of risks were conducted as project outputs were defined. The proposal development process was supported by a specially constituted National Social and Environmental Screening Team (NSSET). A risk assessment is conducted by members of civil society working in the NS2 to review and confirm the description of risk identified during the screening exercise in March 2018. The mitigation measures have been incorporated into the Outputs and Activities.

199. The main risks relate to setting up appropriate governance structures and contractual arrangements to ensure that the project is implemented effectively, efficiently and sustainably over the entire project period. As communities will lead, manage and implement outputs 1-3, arrangements should ensure that they are able to do so in a manner consistent with their own values and practices. It is important that benefits distributed equitably amongst stakeholders. The legal basis to secure access to timber and other resources must be established. In addition, the project must ensure that appropriate governance processes and capacity are put in place to comply with good practice guidelines, codes and management practices and all groups within the communities are engaged, barriers, governance and technical risk is addressed.

200. Risks regarding appropriate fire management techniques are deemed to be lower as an adequate budget and relevant expertise has been accounted for; however cultural practices by some groups (such as the Ewe) may result in fires being set on cropland. In addition, if land use decisions are not set. Cropland, grassland and degraded land that will be reforested have to be identified through participatory processes. This is important so that they can be managed with appropriate technical capacity in place. Areas designated for plantations must conform to biodiversity conservation and sustainable development principles. If areas of forest are converted from one use to another or allocated for a different purpose as a result of a policy or practices and further action may be needed to reconcile optimal land use. Moving from open-access to strong community management of lands, may result in some conflicts with existing tenure arrangements. This could have significant social and economic effects on this. Environmental risks include adequate rainfall for species to grow and availability of water sources. The timing and location of deployment and for reversals of carbon emission reduction needs to be taken into account.

201. The mitigation measures include:
- The project will ensure that FC, district assemblies, private sector companies, communities and women and men farmers will have access to forums, resources, expertise and know-how; therefore technical, structural and administrative factors will be addressed.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 79 OF 83

## Stakeholder Engagement Process

A stakeholder engagement process is defined so that no groups are marginalized and a pervasive redress mechanism allow consensus building and solutions to be identified where there are conflicting interests.

Implementation of the revised ESFM, with application of UNDP SES guidance, as well as adaptive management through the integrated monitoring framework of the GSLM, will ensure that all stakeholders have an opportunity to participate in the process. This is built on a relationship of trust and dialogue with FC, traditional authorities, district staff and private sector agents. The ESFM will be used to identify key stakeholders and their roles and responsibilities; monitor agreements and ascertain and implement gendered roles and responsibilities.

Ensure that the NSZ is consistent with the land use plans for the region, including the potential for large scale land uses plans initiated by the Northern Development Authority recognises and engages the regional land use planning process and involve FC, CREMA government and other stakeholders in the development of the NSZ.

Engage with current policy reform on tree tenure on both off-reserve and on-reserve lands to enable the NSZ to be consistent with the new policy. This will also include engagement with fees, shea and other NTFPs.

Conduct a capacity building and training in the NSZ for FC, Shea producers (women), farmers, land use planners at the district level, fire managers, extension workers at the district level, and local government officials. The roles and responsibilities will be defined. Strong community governance and institutions will be the hallmark of this process.

An awareness, communication and extension plan to seek the best way to carry out activities and contribute adaptive management to ensure that changes in implementation are based on needs and priorities.

Integrate capacity building for the Shea Unit in COCODOMI, MOFA, CRIS in Boke (for research purposes) and other stakeholders as appropriate.

Ensure collaboration with the Sustainable Land Management project and the efforts made for water harvesting and soil conservation. The NSZ will be used to identify areas suitable for ground preparation and plastic bottle technology. Projects that are already underway relevant for the NSZ and feed these into the national SIS; providing information relating to the social and environmental standards and safeguards.

Use of innovative, accessible and effective approaches, to monitor land use change and carbon emissions reductions as a result of project activities not only within the project areas but for the entire project area.

## Project Financial Details

A detailed analysis of the environmental and social risks of the project and the related management plan is presented in Annex 6. UNDP's SEEP requires that no activities that may cause adverse social and environmental impacts are undertaken without a thorough risk assessment. The revised ESFM, adoption of appropriate mitigation and management measures are completed. It is suggested that for the ESA consultants, together with stakeholders, create a list of such possible activities that is available to all stakeholders and respected by the various governance and regulatory bodies of the project.

## Project Financial Details

Project financial details include: completion of all social and environmental risk assessments (including the prompt completion of the ESIA), the elaboration of the proposed ESFM and associated management plans (including the review and possible modification of the Gender Assessment and Action Plan – Annex 8), and the adoption and readiness of all recommended mitigation measures. The project will not proceed until each mitigation measure may be a pre-condition to the carrying out of any specific project activity.

G.2 Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

There is capacity of the Forestry Commission and the Global Shea Alliance to implement the ESM and other arrangements for compliance monitoring, supervision and reporting are in place and budgets set aside for the required assessments.

Proposed gender targets to implement recommendations

Target 1: Build capacity of women and provide them with adequate resources (e.g. technology, training, access to how, etc.) to play a key role effectively in REDD+ implementation.

Target 2: Rightfully include women as stakeholders in CREAMA and market forest products and associated benefits within the CREAMA and MTS are agreed to by women and men

Target 3: Ensure that women are meaningfully engaged in all capacity building and awareness raising activities and decision-making processes

Target 4: Ensure that women are represented on the PSC, REDD+ steering committee, consultation, workshop, etc.

Target 5: Provide a gender perspective to be provided to build capacities on how to mainstream gender in REDD+ implementation among involved agencies (e.g. FCD, REDD+ institutions and structures, and project users)

Target 6: G’s Gender Sub-Working Group is integrated into the institutional structure for the project and is on the PSC

Target 7: A gender budget to implement gender-related activities is allocated and a gender perspective integrated into mid-term and terminal evaluations with particular using the “W” Approach.

Target 8: A gender perspective is integrated into data collection, monitoring and reporting

There may be other ways in which such a cross-cutting and integrated approach on gender will not only help to address the identified gender gaps and barriers, but also help to ensure effective and equitable inclusion of women in REDD+ implementation. The project will collect baseline information about gender issues can on other components of the programme and will be integrated as such. In the monitoring phase, data collected from the baseline survey (including age disaggregated) and age disaggregated data will be collected, with the goal of measuring both the numerical impact and positive or negative impacts on women’s empowerment, including their access to land and men’s (and youth, when applicable) perception and livelihoods as a result of the project. This breadth of indicators will allow for an assessment of the gender impacts of REDD+ implementation, the areas of improvement and allow the project to monitor changes and gender impacts in order to have them feed back into decision-making and planning.

G.3 Financial management and procurement (max. 200 words, approximately 1 page)

The financial management and procurement of this project will be guided by UNDP financial rules and regulations available here: UNDP Financial Rules and Regulations

**Page 85**

209. Further guidance is outlined in the financial resources management section of the UNDP Programme and Operations Policies and Procedures available at [Financial Resources Management](#).

210. UNDP has comprehensive procurement policies as laid out in the 'Contracts and Procurement' section of UNDP's Programme and Operations Policies and Procedures (POP). The policies are consistent with the United Nations Common Procurement Guidelines for the procurement process, and they apply to all procurements in UNDP. See - [Procurement](#).

211. The project will be implemented following the National Implementation Modality (NIM) following NIM guidelines and procedures.

212. The NIM guidelines are a formal part of UNDP's policies and procedures and were corporately developed and adopted by UNDP and are fully compliant with UNDP's procurement and financial management rules and regulations.

213. The national executing entity FC (also referred to as the national Implementing Partner or UNDP technical partner) will manage the project in compliance with UNDP rules and regulations, policies and procedures (including the NIM guidelines). In legal terms, this is ensured through the governance structure of the project, which includes a Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) signed with a UNDP project document which will be signed by the Implementing Partner to govern the use of the funds. The national executing entity FC, as well as the implementing partner, will be referred to as the national Implementing Partner, needs to have undergone a Harmonized Approach to Cash Transfer (HACT) framework training and certification in accordance with UNDP guidelines. During implementation, UNDP will provide oversight and quality assurance in accordance with its policies and procedures. The project will be subject to an annual performance assessment (APA) and project confirmation to be agreed with the GCF. This may include, but is not limited to, monitoring missions, spot checks, facilitation and participation in project board meetings, quarterly progress and annual financial reports, site visits, and other activities as required by UNDP based on the resources reserved from received.

214. UNDP认识到实施伙伴的能力建设对于从现金转移方案到实施伙伴（参见HACT框架）的过程至关重要。该过程由四个阶段组成，即：i) 宏观评估；ii) 微观评估；iii) 项目管理计划和工作计划；iv) 持续监测。这些评估应包括规划、定期在线审查（检查点）、程序性监控、计划的审计和特殊报告。实施伙伴必须在每个阶段完成所有要求，以确保其能力得到充分发展，并且当发现差距时，需要纠正这些差距。如果未解决这些问题，则可能意味着实施伙伴的能力不足，从而导致更高的风险和更大的挑战。

215. The project will be audited in accordance with UNDP policies and procedures on audits, informed by and together with any specific requirements agreed in the AMA. According to the current audit policy of UNDP, the audit will be conducted at least once a year during the implementation programme cycle as per UNDP assurance/sustainability plans, on the basis of the implementing partner's risk rating category. The audit findings will be shared with the GCF and the implementing partner. Any recommendations to the implementing partner were used for the appropriate purpose and in accordance with the work plan, and any corrective actions taken by the implementing partner or as a result of the audit.

216. All GCF resources will be provided to the executing entity, less any agreed cost recovery amount. Under UNDP's national implementation modality, UNDP advances cash funds on a quarterly basis in accordance with the approved project budget and work plan. The executing entity is responsible for ensuring that all project tasks expenditure via a financial report on a quarterly basis to UNDP. Any additional requirements will be as in accordance with the AMA as and when it is agreed.

G.4. Disclosure of funding proposal

- No confidential information: The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be disclosed in full by the GCF, as no information is being provided in confidence.
- With confidential information: The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may not be disclosed in full by the GCF, as certain information is being provided in confidence. According to the accredited entity's policy on providing to the Secretariat the following two copies of the funding proposal, including all annexes:
  - Full copy for internal use of the GCF in which the confidential portions are marked accordingly, together with a cover letter from the accredited entity stating the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the accredited entity’s disclosure policy.
  - Redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website.

The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential information.

RE: FUNDING PROPOSAL FOR THE GCF BY UNITED NATIONS DEVELOPMENT PROGRAMME ON GHANA SHEA LANDSCAPE EMISSION REDUCTION PROJECT

We refer to the "Ghana Shea Landscape Emission Reduction Project" as included in the funding proposal submitted by UNDP – Ghana to the Republic of Ghana-NDA in March 2020.

The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of the Ministry of Finance, the National Designated Authority/Focal point of the Republic of Ghana.

Pursuant to GCF decision B/5810, the content of which we acknowledge to have reviewed, we hereby communicate our no-objection to the Ghana Shea Landscape Emission Reduction Project as included in the funding proposal.

By communicating our no-objection, it is implied that:

(a) The government of the Republic of Ghana has no objection to the project proposal as included in the amended funding proposal;

(b) The project proposal as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with the Republic of Ghana's national priorities, strategies and plans; and

(c) In accordance with the GCF's environmental and social safeguards the project as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with relevant national laws and regulations.

This no-objection letter supersedes the earlier one we issued on the subject "Ghana Shea Landscape Emission REDD+ Project" in 2018.

We also confirm that our national process for ascertaining no-objection to the Ghana Shea Landscape Emission Reduction Project as included in the funding proposal has been duly followed.
