Secretariat’s assessment of FP137

Proposal name: Ghana Shea Landscape Emission Reductions Project

Accredited entity: United Nations Development Programme (UNDP)

Country/ies: Ghana

Project/programme size: Medium

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| Strong community development, investment planning and creation of local development forest funds | Securing strong buy-in from the shea market - meeting future demands |
| Has strong gender focus with significant level of empowerment in the shea value chain |  |
| Opportunity to pilot an innovative restoration mechanism for shea parklands through performance-based agreements (Global Shea Alliance) and scalability to the entire Sabe Zone for shea production |  |

* The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions set out in annex XIII short and addendum XVIII, titled "List of proposed conditions and recommendations".

## Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### Project background
1. The Ghana Shea Landscape Emission Reductions project (GSLERP) aims to restore degraded landscapes and strengthen the livelihoods in the northern savannah zone (NSZ) through enhanced ecosystem services.
2. The project seeks to enhance forest carbon stocks across the landscape in the NSZ through the following interventions:
   - Degraded shea parklands are identified, mapped, fenced off from grazing lands and placed under self-financing community management in community resource management areas (CRMAs).
   - Restoring 100,000 hectares of degraded shea parklands.


---
**Page 94**
---

(i) Creating 25,000 hectares of modified laungua system (MTS)/forest plantation in overall degraded forest reserves;
(ii) Implementing an integrated monitoring system and completing the national REDD-plus architecture for safeguards, forest monitoring and reporting systems.

The project area is the NSE region of the Democratic Republic of Congo – with an area of 9.7 million hectares, dominated by savannah forest and grassland, and which is also inhabited by a significant diversity of species that are only found in the savannah ecological zones. The forest covers areas where agriculture has been practiced for centuries, including those occurring naturally in traditional farming systems. The area covers three administrative regions, namely the northern region (Nord), the southern region (Sud) and the eastern region (Est). It is located in the zone of the country. With a population of over five million people, the NSE region is the poorest region in the country. The majority of its inhabitants are small-scale farmers who depend on agriculture for their livelihoods. Over 90 per cent of rural households engaged in rain-fed agricultural activities without alternative income sources and with highly varied rainfall patterns increase the vulnerability and lower agricultural productivity.

Shea landscapes are important sources of carbon storage and sequestration both above ground and below ground, as well as providing a range of ecosystem services such as water provision, providing essential products and ecological services to this drier region. Shea production is beneficial for rural households in terms of food security, income generation, enhancing resilience and providing a buffer in ecosystem services through tree retention (e.g., water and soil retention, prevention of desertification). Shea, being a major source of economic livelihoods, for women in particular, is also an important source of income. However, the collection of shea fruits coincides with the "lean" season of the year before the agricultural season.

A significant increase in temperature and excessive droughts have been observed in the region, contributing to a decrease in soil fertility and soil water retention capacity. Transitions in the agro-ecological zones are also being observed as a result of climate change. The use of mechanized ploughing for certain crops (e.g., maize) – and facilitation of subsistence farming through the introduction of improved seeds, fertilizers, pesticides, planting trees and eliminating regeneration. Heavy reliance on wood for charcoal production in the region has led to deforestation and degradation of forest resources. In addition, degraded savanna lands also raise the need for sustainable fuel wood production from forest areas. Logging and mid-to-large scale agriculture are also contributing factors to land degradation. This increases the growth of grasses that are resulting in more intense fires in the dry season. It is estimated that 100,492 hectares have been deforested, resulting in emissions of 3,546 million CO2e per year.

The total project finance needed is USD 54.55 million, with a request to GOP for grant finance of USD 38.75 million. The Government of the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC) is contributing USD 10.15 million in the form of grants (in-kind), and private sector companies are contributing a total of USD 9 million in the form of grants (in-kind). UNDP is also providing USD 3.5 million in the form of grants (in-kind). The environmental and social safeguards category of the project is B.

1 The MTS has evolved from the Tanaqua system that was practiced extensively between the 1960 and 1980s to rehabilitate degraded forest areas. It was developed by the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry in collaboration with the World Bank as part of the National Forest Plantation Development Programme in 2005, by opening up additional land for agriculture and reforestation. The MTS is a community-based participatory approach that combines traditional agroforestry practices with a collaborative forest restoration method to help provide livelihood opportunities for forest communities, while also affording them access to the benefits of their forests. It involves the establishment of a reserve on private lands where farmers conduct farming practices on reserve in return for providing labour to plant trees and subsequently maintain them.

# Component-by-component analysis

## Component 1: Off-reserve degraded savannah forests restored under and financing community-managed CREAMA (total cost: USD 73.6 million; GCF cost: USD 105.789 million or 42.5 per cent)

* This component will support the restoration of degraded forest and land-use management system and increase the adaptive capacities of the local communities. It will place 200,000 hectares of degraded savannah forests under CREAMA and will reduce CO2 emissions by 1,271,899 tCO2e for the project duration of seven years; and there will be additional 225,000 hectares where local communities will engage in improved fire management. This component will also provide USD 3 million to CREAMA per year for the project duration.

* The proposed interventions under this component will support strengthening and developing CREAMA as the management groups responsible for establishing sustainable charcoal and wood fuel production systems within the forest areas.

### Component 1a: Off-reserve degraded savannah forests restored (public-private partnership) (total cost: USD 19.36 million; GCF cost: USD 10.485 million, or 53.7 per cent)

* This component focuses on supporting the restoration of 180,000 hectares of degraded shea pastures and other land uses such as agriculture and other trees. A total of 1.75 million shea trees will be restocked in the parklands with higher and quicker-yielding varieties of shea, and additional 243,693 hectares of degraded savannah forests will be restored under CREAMA. The future supply of shea through performance-based contracts between members of the forest area association and buyers will be increased by USD 10 million per year as a result of this partnership between the communities and the buyers. Increasing the economic value of shea trees is expected to change the incentive system for tree protection.

* 11. The proposed interventions under this component will support increasing awareness of tree planting opportunities, establishing shea nurseries, improving extension services to support tree planting activities, and promoting agroforestry systems such as intercropping with fallow systems. Particular focus will be on supporting the establishment of women's collectives to function as processing hubs, which is expected to increase the adaptive capacity of 20,000 women in the project area by providing them with improved access to markets for shea, access to new technologies, aggregation in warehouses and direct sales to processors for higher prices.

* 12. The proposed interventions are expected to reduce carbon emissions by 58,529 tCO2e from shea planting and 264,631 tCO2e from other trees, with a further 163,133 tCO2eq from reduction in deforestation.

## Component 3: Modified tusker system plantation and fire management forest reserves (total cost: USD 27.5 million; GCF cost: USD 16.67 million or 48.9 per cent)

* This component will support the establishment of 10,000 hectares of new plantations as well as two protected areas covering 512,200 hectares, of which 455,700 hectares are in Moi National Park and 56,500 hectares in Kipketer Forest Reserve. The objective is to restore degraded reserves are degraded and deforested; and by applying MTS this component aims to provide a mechanism for cost-effective plantation development while increasing community ownership of the forest areas and reducing the incidence of illegal logging and other forms of encroachment in the reserves.

* 14. The proposed interventions under this component will support commercial plantation development in the NZI has been challenging due to limited access to finance and lack of incentives to protect the forest reserves. Under this component, the interventions will support a programme of MTS

# Assessment of performance against investment criteria

## Impact potential Scale: High

The project has a significant impact potential as a cross-cutting project. Through the improved forest management and sustainable land use practices, it will contribute to reduced greenhouse gas emissions from deforestation and forest degradation. These contributions are significant, amounting to a reduction in 25,874,910 tCO₂eq over 20 years (assuming a discount rate of 3% for 20 years' duration). This impact is likely to be further amplified by strengthening the CREMA and their key role in restoring and conserving Shea parkslands, improved fuelwood production and other forest products.

The project will contribute to the restoration of degraded savanna and Shea parklands. A significant impact is that, once restored, these lands will carry on acting as safety nets and main livelihoods for communities living within or close to the project area.

The project will directly strengthen the livelihoods and climate resilience of vulnerable communities and strongly support them in gaining strengthened capacities of forest management, fuelwood production, fire management and access to finance through the CREMA structures.


---
**Page 97**
---

3.2 Paradigm shift potential

The project will help to restore and conserve the landscapes that contain millions of rural people in Ghana. The project will also play a very innovative role in creating Shea parklands and securing the future production of shea nuts. The piloted supported across the components will enable Ghana to scale up the interventions in other parts of the country.

The activities developed by the project, with the involvement of local communities and involved in the restoration and conservation efforts of the degraded shea parklands and savannah landscapes is general. Through the CREMA support, communities will build key capacity and improve their livelihoods through improved access to markets and access to finance.

This project is a very innovative basis on which to secure the future of the Shea landscapes. Furthermore, the involvement of the GSA will help experience that can later be scaled up and used in the development of a regional area landscape programme.


3.3 Sustainable development potential

The activities developed by the project, with the involvement of local communities, will directly contribute to protecting and conserving the environment that sustains the livelihoods of people living in the Shea landscapes. The project will also help to conserve important biodiversity and secure flow of ecosystem services to the rural poor.

The nature of the activities will enable the communities to diversify their livelihoods and gain key experience on how best to manage and protect the resources within the shea parklands. This will also have very important socioeconomic impacts beyond the project area.


3.4 Needs of the recipient

The project targets one of the poorest and most climate vulnerable areas in Ghana, the NSR where Shea parklands exist. The project will help to improve the livelihoods of people in these landscapes by addressing some of their key environmental challenges such as the shea parklands and support communities in improving their forest management skills and practices.


Furthermore, the project will help to create long-term transformation of community organizations through its support of the CREMA which will be able to adapt to new challenges and demands in the future due to the experience gained this project.

3.5 Country ownership

Country ownership is considered high. The Government prioritizes development of the NSR and adjacent areas. The project is fully aligned with the country’s determined contributions and numerous strategies in the forest and ecosystem sectors, and is part of the GEF grant guide for Ghana that has both northern and the Shea parklands.

There is a very strong partnership from national institutions and local communities with the Forestry Commission comprise the executing entity (EE), full ownership is with the Government.

3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

Scale: High

11. The project is financed through grants and its lead contributions, but the nature of the project and the fact that it will be implemented in a region with significant private sector finance, means that the project can help to foster an enabling environment for the private sector. Shea butter is an important ingredient in many food and cosmetic products and, through the partnership between the GCF and the private sector, the project has the potential to increase its production and leverage additional private sector finance.

# Gender policy

The AE has provided a gender assessment and gender action plan, and therefore complies with the requirements of the Gender Policy of the GCF.

## 42.1 Gender policy

### 37. The AE's gender assessment indicates the country's commitment to gender equality and women's empowerment. Ghana has signed international conventions such as the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) which prohibits discrimination based on gender, and the country has a National Gender Policy (2015) that provides a framework for integrating gender considerations into the national development process. Further, Ghana has frameworks and plans, such as the national medium-term development plan, and the affirmative action plan with a 60 per cent quota for women in public service.

### 38. Although the enabling environment exists, implementation of the various policies, plans and frameworks is hampered by gender inequalities that are deeply rooted in customary and statutory laws on marriage, divorce, inheritance, custody and land tenure have significant implications on access, use and control of resources by women and men. Existing barriers to achieving gender equality include: low levels of education for girls; strong adherence to socio-cultural and traditional beliefs; inadequate basic social services; low representation of women in decision-making positions at all levels of government; women's earnings are less educated, and are excluded from access to land or ownership of land. The lack of ownership of land by women is a major barrier to their economic empowerment.

### 39. Data on the number of female-headed households in Ghana is limited. However, data and information provided in the funding proposal indicate that women in Ghana face issues in accessing resources and services, training and participating in decision-making.


---
**Page 100**

product market. Shea trees are located far from their homes, so women will have to travel long distance, facing dangers on the way and work in competitive environments when collecting the shea nuts. Women have limited information on market prices and their immediate need for cash is often greater than their ability to wait until a better price comes along or whatever price is on offer. Moreover, women operate individually at a small scale and are not leveraging economies of scale through action which could improve their efficiency and bargaining power, and could help to regulate the market.

### 4.1 Gender action plan describes activities that will address the challenges faced by women in shea production and marketing. The gender action plan is an integral part of all GCF projects. All will put in place a gender support to support the implementation of the gender action plan, and the expert will work with the project team and other groups involved in the implementation of the gender action plan. The gender action plan includes various activities that are linked to the findings of the assessment but also indicates the need to conduct further assessments (e.g., the need for additional gender-sensitive market analysis, the need to engage the community, and to ensure that further issues are addressed (e.g., the need for additional gender-sensitive training on business, contract and warehouse management and direct marketing), build women's skills and capacity in decision-making, and the need to explore the use of new technologies (fuel- and water-efficient processing technologies and quality control) to increase efficiency and increase the income (by 30–50 per cent) of women engaged in shea production while also increasing their participation in decision-making. The gender action plan will ensure their enhanced participation and capacity in decision-making.

### 4.3 Risks

#### 4.3.1 Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

The GCF is requested to provide a grant of USD 30.1 million to enhance forest carbon stocks in Ghana through the restoration and rehabilitation of degraded forests and agroforestry systems in Ghana. The Forestry Commission (the ER) and multiple private sector companies are providing co-financing of USD 15 million and USD 9 million, respectively, by way of grants and in-kind contributions.

It is expected that the project will lead to revenue generation from the sale of timber, wood, non-timber forestry products, shea trees and MTS plantation. Despite the revenue-generating potential of the project, there are several risks associated with the implementation of the project and the current state of Ghana's public finances which limit the Government's ability to take on even concessional debt.

### 4.3.2 Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current programme (medium risk)

#### e1. UNDP is the AE for this project. The AE has had representation in the country since 1978 and has been playing a leading role in supporting the mainstreaming of climate change and environment considerations into development planning in the country.

The Ministry Government is also the EE of the project. It has established offices at the regional and district level. Over the past ten years, the EE has experience in implementing projects with a total value of USD 15 million and an average annual budget of USD 1.5 million and USD 0.2 million. The AE carried out the capacity assessment of the EE and the overall rating risk of the EE is low.

### 4.3.3 Programme-specific execution risks (medium risk)

#### e1. Governance for benefit sharing and reinvestment: The project is expected to operate several CREMA from the revenues from the forest, tree plantations and MTS plantation. A community or revolving fund will be set up in each CREMA. The project will set up the process for the selection of the beneficiaries and the criteria for inclusion and exclusion of investment decisions (e.g., sharing of benefits costs) including operation and maintenance, eligibility criteria, etc. This will be done through a Community Resource Management Committee with oversight and guidance from the EE, an advisory group and the project team. The CREMA will have a board of directors that will be elected by community signatures on a local bank account, and frequent oversight and auditing of accounts by the CREMA Executive Committee and a third party will be in place. It is critical that the AE/EE carry out regular monitoring to ensure that these governance structures are functioning as intended and that these governance structures before the project implementation. The proposed revolving mechanism for reinvestment of revenues from the forest, tree plantations and MTS plantation will be generated in the medium to long term (especially for Outputs 2 and 3), the project may benefit from co-financing from the private sector at a later stage through a public-private partnership level.

#### e1. Private sector involvement: Output 2 will be implemented via a public-private partnership. Activities to be financed by GCF and the private sector are separated, and the results of these activities will be measured separately using an independent assessment contract. This output is expected to create a long-term relationship between buyers and commercial timber producers in the country. The project will also seek co-financing. A total of USD 1 million of co-financing from the private sector is indicated as per their co-financing letters, which are currently under review by the GCF. It should be noted that this co-financing will only be provided if and when it has been confirmed and will be confirmed prior to the FAA execution.

#### e1. Carbon credit sale: A private sector co-financee may wish to sell carbon credits and present them as a financial return on investment. The project team will need to ensure that any sales of carbon reductions will need to be adjusted accordingly. While the AE clarified that the project does not have any plans to sell carbon credits, it is important for the project team to understand how carbon credits will be counted by the country towards its nationally determined contribution, any sales of carbon credits should be properly monitored to account for effective emission reductions and ensure that they are not double-counted. The integrated monitoring system and strengthened REDD+ plus system should be able to differentiate between emission reductions from forest conservation and those from project counting in the future.

#### e1. Project viability and sustainability: A financial analysis has been carried out for each output, which shows a positive net present value. Output 1 and Output 2 (amounting to 56 per cent of the total project cost) result in the financial internal rates of return of 19 per cent and 48 per cent, respectively. The AE responded that, despite the financial viability

### Legal assessment

1. The AMA was signed with the AE on 5 August 2016, and it became effective on 23 November 2016.

2. The AF has provided a legal opinion/confirmation that it has obtained all internal approvals and has the capacity and authority to implement the project.

3. The proposed project will be implemented in Ghana, a country in which the GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, among other things, the GCF is not protected against claims for damages or compensation arising from its activities. A draft privileges and immunities agreement was sent to the Government of Ghana on 19 November 2016. The GCF has received a letter dated 3 December 2016 regarding ongoing negotiations.

4. The Heads of State Independent Redress Mechanism and Independent Integrity Unit have both expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress activities and/or investigations, as appropriate, in countries where the GCF is not provided with relevant privileges and immunities. Therefore, no claims for damages or compensation against the GCF are made only after the GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation in the country, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of FP137

## Proposal name:
Ghana Shea Landscape Emission Reductions Project

## Accredited entity:
United Nations Development Programme (UNDP)

## Country(list):
Ghana

## Project/programme size:
Medium

### I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

#### 1.1 Impact potential: Scale: Medium to high

- Ghana, with a population of over 29 million people and a surface area of 238,535 km², has very high rates of deforestation and forest degradation.
- Specifically, the proposed project is expected to take place in a fragile ecosystem sensitive to climate change impacts. The region includes the Northern Savannah Zone (NSZ), where the proposed project is located.
- The region has 77 per cent of its forest cover degraded or converted into agricultural land use, with a population of over 5 million people with high poverty levels.
- The region has nearly 400 million trees in severely degraded forest reserves and 1.2 billion trees in moderately degraded forest reserves.

#### 1.2 Project contribution

- The conversion to agriculture, wood fuel harvesting, and charcoal production has been the main drivers of deforestation and degradation in the NSZ.
- This has led to a decline in the number of grasses, which, in turn, results in more intense fires in the dry season, frequently causing further forest degradation and deforestation and preventing the regeneration of forest areas already degraded by fire.
- The proposed project will contribute to enhancing forest carbon stocks across the landscape by:
  - Restoring 200,000 ha of off-reserve savanna forests/woodlands and placing them under community management through the Community Environmental Management Areas (CEMAs).
  - Restoring 100,000 ha of degraded shea parkslands through public-private partnerships.
  - Implementing a forest restoration program in severely degraded forest reserves.
  - Completing the national REDD-plus architecture for safeguards, forest monitoring and reporting systems.

#### 1.3 Financial support

- The GCF funds and co-finance will support women collectors in restoring 275,000 shea trees over an estimated period of 40 years or a project lifetime.
- In addition, 400,000 other high-value trees within CEMAs and agroforestry systems will be planted and nurtured, forming a wide range of ecosystem services, timber, and non-timber products.

#### 1.4 Emission reductions

- These actions will deliver 6,139 MCO2e in emission reductions and removals over the first seven years of the project.
- The proposed project on emissions reductions from the forest sector across NSZ have been estimated based on information and data presented primarily from Ghana’s forest reference level.


---
**Page 107**
---

submitted to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC)
secretariat in January 2017 and subsequently revised in June 2017.

6. However, there is a high risk that these estimates could be overestimated. The NSE area has been facing several challenges for years, as stated above, including fires, droughts, overgrazing, and the reduction of the natural regeneration available on water. This risk will increase as climate change progresses.
- In 2015, the Ghana Forestry Commission (GFC) selected a subarea within the NSE for an estimate of the water balance to sustain the tree plantations, to which the response was that only seedlings in the nursery would be watered for two years and that sufficient water can be obtained from rainfall. The GFC has also stated that there is no evidence of natural regeneration that exist in the landscape. Transplanted seedlings and farmer-managed natural regeneration will be required to sustain the tree plantations.
- Proponents have indicated that several studies show that there will be sufficient groundwater to sustain the tree plantations, but these studies are based on climate change scenarios. Water scenarios will play a crucial role in ensuring success or failure in terms of tree survival together with the above-notioned actions; it is crucial to have adequate water balance estimates.
8. In terms of tree survival, the proponents state that their projection includes a 50 per cent mortality rate for the first two years and 250 per cent mortality rate for the subsequent targeted for planting and budgeted for was 3.5 million trees plus additions from natural regeneration (GFC, 2016). They have also made provision for a mortality of 20 per cent on the non-tree shea.
9. In terms of forest fires the proponents explain that the objective is to repair destructive roads in the NSE area and to build firebreaks. The project will not have any negative impact on forest regeneration and will help to reduce the effects of climate change currently experienced by the communities. However, the project will be implemented in the first month of the dry season, so fire management capacities in the form of fire brigades are in place.
10. A REDD+ plus investment plan is currently being developed at the country level. The project proposal expects to contribute to Ghana’s national approach to REDD-plus through the implementation of a number of activities that will be implemented over time and will enable the attainment of Ghana’s global mitigation role through its nationally determined contribution. However, this is just one small component of the overall project, aimed basically to integrate forest management into land use planning in order to establish a baseline reference level and land cover maps to develop a clear assessment of past and current trends in forest cover change. The project will also provide an opportunity for Ghana to host data, maps and other information which can be accessed by key stakeholders.
11. Ghana is assessing its eligibility to receive results-based payments from the NSE and other projects that are being implemented under the framework of the REDD-plus architecture of cooperative and voluntary approaches and the mechanisms established through Article 6 of the Paris Agreement. The project proposal does not include any specific reference to any concrete actions or estimates, as the REDD-plus architecture is under development.
12. The project proposes adaptation benefits, as actions will be supporting poverty alleviation and food security for communities in the NSE area and its surroundings, positively affecting 106,200 direct beneficiaries (79,850 women and 21,350 men) and 546,200 indirect beneficiaries. The project also proposes adaptation benefits as described above, but as explained in sections 1.2 and 4 below on the paradigm shift and effectiveness and efficiency of activities, it is important to note that the number of people whose livelihoods need to be better estimated to ensure medium- and long-term adaptation benefits to communities.


---
**Page 108**
---

11. Overall, the combined mitigation outputs to restore savannah forests, shea plantations and MFT forest plantations, together with the adaptation impacts, have a medium to high potential impact. It should be noted that during the initial years of the project, communities will continue to harvest timber from their own forest reserves and other areas where they grow trees and other productive trees grown to ensure their livelihoods. Since there is a potential for the remaining forests in CREMA areas to be converted into plantations, it is important to plan for viable sustainable options for the initial years and pay attention to the overall sustainability of the project interventions.

# 1.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: Medium to high

## 1.3 Innovation

### 1.3.1 Community Resource Management Areas (CREMAS)

14. The project's first component focuses on supporting 10 CREMAS, groups of communities that have agreed to work together for the management of shared natural resources under locally defined rules and regulations, which are backed by community constitutions and management plans, leading to official recognition. Their main activities will include the production of non-timber forest products (NTFPs) such as shea nuts, honey, and fruits, limiting the harvesting of forest products and timber outside CREMA and forest reserves. The sustainability of these communities is expected to be enhanced by the creation of a number forest products and timber that will provide livelihood diversification opportunities to communities. CREMAS will also be responsible for tree management with the support of the Forest Services Division.

15. The project proposal explains that CREMA development is not a rapid or easy process and that it requires time, commitment, and resources. It proposes to establish an economic base with an equitable sharing scheme. The project is therefore proposing the creation of community CREMA funds. It is proposed that each community fund in each CREMA be significantly funded by the communities themselves through their own income-generating activities that can be replicated in other CREMAS and that the CREMA Executive Committee would manage these funds. The money generated from these income-generating activities that the CREMAS will be undertaking will be used to capitalize the funds. Money earned from the sale of timber, shea nuts, honey, fruits, etc., will be deposited into the GCF resources will not be used to contribute to the community funds, there could be a potential risk of overharvesting and depletion of forest resources.

16. The second project component will support women collectors in restoring 17.5 million shea trees in the plantations. Shea trees have been established in the NSZ reserve for many years and are now ready to be harvested. This will ensure that there is a sustainable supply of shea varieties of shea to ensure future supply to enter commercial contracts between members of the Global Shea Alliance (GSA). The project proposes to establish 10,000 hectares of plantations will be planted within agroforestry systems. Linkages between shea cooperatives that use wood fuel and wood fuel operators will strengthen local demand for sustainable wood fuel.

17. The third component focuses on the establishment of MFT forest plantations in the tanzania forest plantations. NSZ contains 66 forest reserves covering an area of 63,800 ha as well as two protected areas (the Ngorongoro Conservation Area and the Serengeti National Park) where they are degraded. For output 3 the Forestry Commission will establish MFT plantations on deforested portions of 12 forest reserves that have been identified by the Forestry Commission and for which there is a demand from communities. The project proposes to establish 7,000 hectares of MFT forest plantations with the Forestry Commission will invest in restoring a further 7,000 ha with direct contracts with the

1. **5 million trees** will be planted in the plantations and in CREMAS and it is expected that, at a minimum, half of these will survive at the end of seven years.

12.2. **Potential for knowledge and learning and enabling environment**

The project has a high potential to create an enabling environment for knowledge generation and sharing among communities through collective benefits and capacity-building activities with communities under the CREMA model.

10. The project will focus on capacity-building to support the aggregation of Shea kernels and direct marketing through performance-based payment contracts for NGOs and private sector companies to deliver services and engage with women's cooperatives and farmers. However, it is important that this model is not only about money but also about knowledge, as it can be more effectively delivered through training of trainers, bearing in mind that the communities have experience of living in forests and know the Shea business and could well be their own best trainers.
11. The project will build on the experience of creating the CREMA model, which was designed to ensure the sustainable use of forest resources by providing a framework for land reforms to enable the model to be used in forest and tree tenure and benefit-sharing. It will be interesting to see how this model ensures the rights associated with community 'rights to trees', which also relates to land tenure.
12. In terms of community forest plantations and their benefits under MTS, the taungya farmers have been trained on best practices for agroforestry systems, including intercropping and sensitization of farmers. Periodic technical training for farmers is intended to be provided by both the Forestry Commission and the Ministry of Agriculture. Through a proposed learning platform 13,000 farmers will be able to share knowledge and experience to effectively manage their forests.
12.2 Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies
14. Scalability and replicability
15. The project will support the structuring of the CREMAS with a participative governance arrangement, which includes the CREMA to define land use and investment planning, develop a strategy and set of activities (management plan) for the CREMA, and define the appropriate benefit-sharing mechanism. The success of the model in terms of its ability to scale up will heavily depend on its governance and managerial success and the financial sustainability during and after project implementation.
16. The scalability of the Shea plantations and the maintenance of the Shea value chain will depend on the arrangements between private companies and communities with equitable sharing of benefits. This is a key issue that needs to be addressed in order to ensure the viability of the country to ensure the architecture of the REDD+ plus scheme and the capacity to negotiate viable carbon credits for the benefit of both the communities and the private sector.
17. The project will establish pathways to scale landscape restoration across NSE and engage multi-stakeholders to determine and implement the long-term vision for a climate smart Shea industry in Ghana.

### 13 Sustainable development potential
#### Scale: High

18. Environmental co-benefits
19. Restoration of landscapes with tree planting will contribute to the retention of soil moisture and the reduction of evapo-transpiration, and support soil fertility. The greater number


---
**Page 111**
---

of trees will also improve water catchment and will contribute to the enhancement of water cycles.

34. Pollinating insects in West Africa depend on flowering trees as a source of food and shelter. When an area is cleared of most of its trees, leaving only species of economic importance, pollinators will be forced into areas where they are not needed. These populations might fall. The remaining trees could struggle to find pollinators for their flowers, in which case the fruit production would also decrease.

35. Biodiversity improvement will also be an important co-benefit, as more diverse trees will help improve soil fertility and water retention. Landscape restoration will support natural habitats allowing a resurgence of savannah forest species, including wildlife. This will increase the provision of non-timber forest products. Fire control will be critical to improve biodiversity.

36. If the REDD-plus scheme is implemented, it will provide further ecosystem services, allowing communities in West Africa to benefit from the carbon market at a country as whole. The connectivity of natural forests is essential in view of the country’s isolated remnants of forests.


### Economic co-benefits

1. Improved and diversified agricultural production systems will ensure food security and will provide an additional income source for the communities, thus improving their quality of life for the communities. Diversifying crop production will enable the communities to have a more stable income throughout the year.

2. The project will also allow communities with saprotrophic potential to sell their shea kernels directly to companies, which will help to generate a stable income. However, there is a real need to ensure that the benefits of this activity are shared equitably among all actors in the shea business. There are also new possibilities to create co-op enterprises and to establish efficient value chains able to support local economies.

3. The project will allow communities to produce other products which will in turn provide food, alternative wood fuel, medicines, building materials and craft products. Communities will be able to sell these products directly or through the shea marketing company. This will help to allow quotas on the non-timber products. The equitable distribution of benefits from a restored forest landscape will therefore benefit several farmer communities that will in turn protect their forest resources.

40. The project will increase and diversify food production and will allow the communities to have a diversified source of income besides the shea business.


### Social co-benefits

42. The farmers and communities around CREMAs will ensure more food security with the diversification of planting various trees besides shea trees. This will allow them to balance their diets and reduce the risk of malnutrition. In addition, the project will help to improve the same time communities will be more resilient to the effects of climate change as forest agroforestry systems are more resistant than monoculture crops.

43. The project will also provide a source of income for women in selected towns.


---
**Page 112**
---

The CREMA system will allow the communities to have a better government system and to manage themselves to take care of their own ecosystems. The model will also promote social cohesion and will support equitable benefit-sharing. Communities will also learn more about land rights and how to use them.

The project will develop communication and information tools that will help the communities to understand the importance of the efforts made to restore and develop more efficient forest landscapes.

The project will also support the involvement of women in managing the shea cooperatives, diversifying their source of income through sustainable production options.


### 1.3.4 Gender-sensitive development

Living conditions in northern Ghana are difficult for poor agricultural communities that have experienced a decline in traditional production systems. Women have turned to the shea business to complement their incomes, but they still face persistent gender inequalities that limit their access and control over resources.

The production of shea has largely been carried out by women and youth. The production process is strenuous and involves heavy work. The collection of firewood used in the shea kernel processing is also a major burden on women. Shea production requires significant human and financial cost for the local communities and shea producers. Deforestation and the loss of forest cover are associated with the increased use of firewood, which poses a serious health hazard and contributes to greenhouse gas emissions.

Ghana has a Gender and REDD+ Roadmap drafted in 2011 and has included the main vision that all women and men have equal access to resources and give men and women equal opportunities to access, participate in, contribute to and benefit from various REDD-plus policies, programs, measures and funds, and hence contribute to environmental and social sustainability.

The gender action plan was developed during a four-day consultation in August 2019, based on the findings of the baseline study. The plan will support women and men with adequate resources (e.g., technology, financing, know-how) to effectively play a key role in the value chain for shea production and marketing. It also aims at ensuring that women have access, manage, harvest and market forest products and enjoy associated benefits within the CREMA. In addition, it addresses women’s representation of at least 40 per cent in decision-making.

The gender plan is mainly oriented to training, capacity-building and inclusion. Women will be provided with training on shea production and marketing techniques. However, training alone is not sufficient. There is a need to ensure that they have seed money to allow them to start their own businesses or join existing shea cooperatives and to maintain the forest landscapes to allow the theory of change of the project.

According to the funding proposal, the expected income streams created throughout the project period are estimated at USD 10 million for male-headed households and USD 2.5 million for female collectors. The total project is investing USD 30 million and the results for female-headed households seem limited. The independent TAP wonders whether it would be possible to reduce the number of female collectors from 4,876 to 2,910 in the CREMAs, reducing some of the overestimated costs mentioned in section 1.6 below on efficiency and effectiveness.


### 1.4 Needs of the recipient

#### 1.4.5 Vulnerability of the country and vulnerable groups


---
**Page 113**
---

10. Ghana has one of the most stable economies in sub-Saharan Africa and saw a period of rapid economic growth from the turn of the century to 2013 with gross domestic product (GDP) growth rates of between 5 and 6 per cent per annum. According to the World Bank, Ghana continued its strong economic performance during the period 2014-2017.
11. The country is considered to have lower middle income status and made significant progress in the Human Development Index (HDI), rising from 0.596 in 1990 to 0.579 in 2015, which placed it in the medium human development category.

13. The country has a diversified economy driven largely by strong agricultural commodity prices and exports. Agriculture accounts for about one-third of GDP and more than half of the workforce sector has grown over the last ten years, accounting for a large proportion of GDP. Growth within the agriculture sector is dominated by cocoa production which has increased from 10 per cent to 20 per cent from 36 per cent in 2000. The sector employs more than 50 per cent of the workforce and accounts for about one-third of total exports, with a total production estimated at over 4 million tonnes of cocoa beans and cocoa kernels within Ghana with total production estimated to be close to 100,000 tonnes, of which 25,000 tonnes is exported, with the industry being identified as generating approximately USD 30 million in foreign exchange.

16. Even though the agriculture sector is NSE contributes to the national GDP, it is carried out using very traditional methods and rudimentary technology, and with high levels of inequality within the farming community, which has led to a concentration of wealth among one third of those urban areas. Ghana’s agriculture is predominantly smallholder, traditional and ranked.

16. These vulnerabilities, combined with a lack of alternative income opportunities, low levels of literacy (as low as 40 per cent) and low population densities that make the provision of services difficult, have led to high rates of poverty in rural areas. The national poverty rate is over 35 per cent of the population, while poverty rates in excess of 60 per cent in many districts and nearly 10 per cent of households being identified as extremely poor.

17. The country lost over 60 per cent of its primary forest cover between 1950 and the turn of the last century (2.7 million ha). Between 2001 and 2015 the deforestation rate was 3-4 per cent per year, with a total loss of about 800,000 hectares over this period. Forests now account for 66 per cent of Ghana’s average annual forest sector emissions of 6.1 Gt CO2 eq. The main drivers are deforestation and degradation (accounting for approximately 75-90 per cent of emissions), wood fuel (3.5 Mt CO2 eq per annum, or 6 per cent of total emissions) and forest fires.

18. Ghana is highly exposed to climate change and variability, especially in the northern part, where 35 per cent of the land mass of the country is a desert with increasing rates of desertification. The average temperature has increased by about 0.4°C over the last three decades, with measured figures ranging between 24 and 30 °C. The annual rainfall amount in Ghana has decreased over the years from an average of 1500 mm to less than 800 mm per year. There is a high degree of observed temperatures and precipitation in six ecological zones to predict future scenarios; averages for the period between 2046-2065 are projected at 37°C, with 900 mm rainfall compared to 2016 and 2080, respectively. Rainfall levels are likely to reduce by between 1.1 and 3.1 per cent across all the agroecological zones by 2020.

18. Climate change is also increasing forest fires and floods with rural populations in the north exposed to greater vulnerability. There is an increased risk for poor farmers as they are finding it increasingly difficult to access land, water and other resources. The impact of food insecurity in the north is a major threat with 453,000 people being food insecure. In terms of water availability, only 45 per cent of the rural and 70 per cent of the urban population have access to improved drinking water sources.

### 15 Country ownership Scale: High

#### 15.1 Alignment with national climate strategy

**Ghana has been an early mover on climate change**, presenting its first national communication in 2008, ratifying the Kyoto Protocol in 2003 and submitting its second national communication in 2014. The country is also one of the largest emitters of carbon dioxide from land use change and forestry (LUCF) as well as the largest source of carbon emissions in the country — 65.1 per cent in 2012 according to the third national communication.

The National Climate Change Policy Shape a National Climate Change Policy with a particular aim of minimizing the loss of carbon sinks by reducing activities that lead to the destruction of natural ecosystems, especially forests and wetlands. The policy also seeks to promote climate-resilient agricultural programmes that restore degraded forests and ecosystems. Moreover, climate change has been guided by the National Climate Change Master Plan (2015–2020), which outlines projects aligned with the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) in Ghana. These include: (a) reducing wood fuel production, plantation development, conservation of trees through agroforestry and consumption; (b) promoting climate-smart agriculture and sustainable land management for Adaptation Strategy with an emphasis on mainstreaming disaster risk reduction and adaptive actions into the sector.

The National REDD+ Strategy is on a national scale with strategic actions at the subnational landscape level. The proposed project responds to the subnational jurisdictional REDD+ pilot site in the northern region of Ghana, which covers 10,524 km² and forms part of the Shea Landscape of Northern Savannah Woodlands (also known as the Shea Savannah Woodland Landscape). The strategy is aligned with the following components of the National Strategy to: (a) significantly reduce emissions from deforestation and forest degradation, while ensuring sustainable land management and promoting landscape-scale restoration; (b) transform the approaches for the production of Ghana’s major agricultural commodities into climate smart production systems and landscapes; and (c) expand platforms for research and innovation in order to support the implementation of the strategy.

The revised Forest and Wildlife Policy (2012) is the overall sector policy for conserving forest resources, wildlife and their habitats. The CREMA proposed in this project derives from the CREMA designed to protect wildlife resources. Currently the government is passing a Management Bill that will enable the CREMA to be given legal status as a protected area.

Ghana has a Forestry Development Master Plan (2016–2030) that aims to achieve a just, green and prosperous future for Ghana’s forests and forest-dependent communities’ welfare and provides adequate means of livelihoods to all Ghanaians in tandem with the 2012 Forest and Wildlife Policy. The country also has a Forest Plantation Strategy 2016–2040 that seeks to increase the area under plantation by 50 per cent, while ensuring sustainable delivery of economic, social and environmental benefits, with five strategies aligned with the proposed project.

### Capacity of accredited entities and executing entities to deliver

#### a)
The United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) will serve as the accredited entity and executing agency for the project. UNDP is one of the world’s largest brokers of climate change grants for developing countries, with a current portfolio of USD 134 billion in mitigation and adaptation projects. The total value of GCF approved projects under UNDP is approximately $6.7 billion. The project builds on the experience and extensive worldwide forest programmes of UNDP, including the Global Environment Facility (GEF) Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (FCPF), which has approved USD 13 million in funding for REDD+ projects.

GCF has approved several projects, including REDD+ results-based payment for Brazil and Ecuador, with UNDP as the accredited entity.

#### b)
UNDP will support the project through its experience in Reducing Emissions from Deforestation and Forest Degradation. UNDP supports indigenous peoples and forest-dependent communities to participate effectively in national and international efforts to reduce national REDD+ plans and actions that are inclusive and which respect national development priorities as well as the requirements of the UNFCCC process.

UNDP will support the project through its experience in Reducing Emissions from Deforestation and Forest Degradation. UNDP supports indigenous peoples and forest-dependent communities to participate effectively in national and international efforts to reduce national REDD+ plans and actions that are inclusive and which respect national development priorities as well as the requirements of the UNFCCC process.

The executing entity for this project is Ghana’s Forestry Commission, part of the Ministry of Food and Agriculture. The Forestry Commission has a responsibility regulating the utilization of forest and wildlife resources, including fire management and control, the conservation and management of those resources and the coordination of policies related to them. It has also been involved in the development of national REDD+ strategies and will be responsible for coordinating the implementation of the proposed project, relying on its various units and departments as well as working closely with relevant stakeholders.

The Forestry Commission has vast experience in planning and implementing projects and programmes relating to forest land use, including biodiversity conservation, wildlife management, natural resources and environmental governance. It has managed resources above USD 10 million from the Global Environment Facility (GEF) Forest Carbon Partnership Program (USD 7.8 million), the Northern Savanna Biodiversity Conservation Project (USD 2.2 million) and the Sustainable Shea Initiative (USD 35 million). The Forestry Commission also received funds from a number of sources including the Global Environment Facility (GEF) and the Wildlife Management Project (USD 12 million) funded by the Government of the Netherlands.

The project will be implemented under the Ghana Forest Carbon Partnership Program being funded by the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (USD 4.5 million) and the African Development Bank (AfDB) (USD 30 million). ### Engagement with civil society organizations and other relevant stakeholders

The project presents a summary of consultation and a stakeholder consultation plan.

78\. Consultations were also developed with shea private sector actors through a series of discussions with GSA in 2017 and 2018. Early interest, particularly from the buyers and NGOs, enabled the TFSI to be established in April 2019. The TFSI has since been restored through public-private partnerships, including activity 2.1 on restoration and sustainable management of shea trees, activity 3.1 on the development and promotion of shea processing at village and industrial scales and activity 2.3 on enhancing an enabling environment for the industry.

80\. These private sector consultations also led to the development of a new activity 2.25 on upgrading industrial processing equipment to reduce greenhouse gas emissions. # Efficiency and effectiveness

## Cost-effectiveness and efficiency

### Scale: Medium

#### a.1.3 Total project budget is USD $54,966,775, from which USD 30,100,000 is expected to be financed by GCF USD 15,839,422 from the Forestry Commission, USD 9,557,153 from the private sector and USD 16,570,200 from other sources.

#### a.1.3 The expected lifetime emission reductions is 25.2 MCO2eq with an estimated cost of USD 2.8 per tonne of CO2eq. The estimated GCF cost per tonne of CO2eq removed is USD 1.19. This is significantly lower than the current market price for carbon credits.

#### a.1.3 There is potential to gain from emission reductions.

#### a.1.3 The total project costs are very high and are heavily on (i) staff salary; (ii) international consultancy services; (iii) travel expenses; (iv) contractual services (including too many meetings with stakeholders); (v) equipment including vehicles and motorcycles, furniture, mobile phones, computers, etc.; (vi) office supplies; (vii) printing costs; (viii) other miscellaneous spending costs. All these expenses appear exaggerated.

#### a.1.3 The project will generate revenue from community management of forests such as timber, non-timber forest products and charcoal, and the sale of surplus agricultural products from the taungya groups and Shea value chain will provide the start-up capital to set up or strengthen existing community-managed enterprises. If these revenues are sufficient to cover resources, the communities will be expected not only not pay for the CREMA management and governance but also generate a profit. This will be used to finance the purchase of fuel wood, tree planting and maintenance or any other green activity to maintain the forests growing in perpetuity.

#### a.1.3 The feasibility study analyses the challenges facing the CREMA model in terms of sustainability and expansion. The first challenge is financial viability, as a CREMA takes significant time to become financially viable. The second challenge is that it costs USD 400,000 to 600,000. Moreover, to maintain CREMAS some cheats have opted to destroy forests for fast cash (including charcoal).

#### a.1.3 According to the proponents, over the past 18 months UNDP and the Secretariat have thoroughly discussed how to set up and incorporate the CREMA fund into the project and ensure that it is sustainable in the long term. The Secretariat has also been working together with other promote sustainability for the project and to enable the community to continue to manage the CREMA fund, which provides a revenue stream from charcoal, fuel wood and non-timber forest products as per their forest management plans.


---
**Page 118**
---

### 90. However at this stage, the project is basically using the GCF grant to establish the CREMA and will ensure the necessary enabling condition for this process, but the question of funding remains.

#### 91. In terms of provisioning that Shea trees normally start fruiting after 7 years and more reliably after 15 years. The expected revenues from plantations will not materialize until the Shea forests and other valuable forests are matured to be able to produce timber and non-timber forest products. This is a very important expectation for the project, which uses techniques (i.e., grafting and other types of vegetative propagation and growth cultures) were used, based on comments from the independent TAP, the expected time frame for Shea trees receiving revenues from only 2 to 6 years. However, based on comments from the independent TAP about the results of the CREMA project in Burkina Faso, which is a similar project with research institutions, UNDP changed the proposal to acknowledge fruiting after 7 years when the project finalizes.

#### 93. Therefore, the means to sustain vast areas of plantations by communities from years 3 to 7 (between the initial period to deliver nurseries and project completion) are rather loose, and the expected revenues from Shea trees will not materialize until they depend on the increased income generated by communities, mainly from shea kernels.

#### 94. Based on three iterations of questions by the independent TAP for the value for money of the project, UNDP has made two minor changes to the original proposal. In response to all the above-mentioned expenses, UNDP came back with two minor changes: (1) they reduced the number of Shea trees per hectare from 2000 to 1500; and in other words they reduced expenses by USD 400,000; and (2) they shifted around USD 1 million in expenses (e.g. The budget of travel was above USD 5.1 million and was reduced to USD 4.9 million). These two changes are expected to have a positive impact on the project, such as the implementation of activities (facilitated by the local business advisory committee), logistics, travel and equipment, and other expenses related to the training of communities regarding techniques and logistics for the brigades to conduct early burning.

#### 95. On the other hand, the independent TAP also asked questions about the benefits that would come from Shea trees in terms of income generation and maintain their livelihoods from years 2 to 7 and the economic estimates of their increased production during these years. The findings are as follows: (1) Farmers will continue to collect kernels from existing trees in the usual way, representing about GHS 58 per woman/year in revenue, increasing to GHS 75 per month after training; (2) Shea farmers who have acquired a good number of seedlings and acquired from training as well as aggregation warehouses. After year 7 when new trees begin to fruit, we expect an increase revenues by GHS 38 per woman/hh, building up to GHS 130 from year 12 onward.

#### 96. Farmers engaged in the project are expected to accrue net benefits from intercropping from year one of the project (GHS 154,000/year) and will continue to do so for years 2-7 with any benefits but a large benefit of GHS 9,838,000 is expected in year 8 from sawn timber, as well as GHS 71,760,000 in year 15.

#### 97. It will be difficult for the independent TAP to judge these estimates, as they depend on the effective restoration of the landscape, the number of people involved in each of the productive systems and the market price of timber. The project is expected to have a long-term economic case once the project action plan is designed with the participation of the communities.

90. In terms of the contribution by the private sector, the project presents letters of co-financing commitment from 14 companies willing to invest in cash and in kind during the 7 years of the project. From these commitments it is worth mentioning that a company called Fosters International Limited is committed to investing USD 350,000 and the Ghana Shea Farmers Association is willing to invest USD 350,000 and World Agroforestry is investing USD 755,000.

91. On the question of the independent TAP on the effective use of these resources to support the project, it was noted that the GCF team has already met with the private sector to discuss the funding for communities through NGOs, adding up all the training already envisaged with the GCF resources and the additional resources from the private sector (USD 390,000) budgeted for warehouse construction and USD 500,000 in purchase seeds.

100. One of the answers to the funding proposal is an operations and maintenance warehouse management plan. The project team has already met with the women's group managing the existing warehouse cooperative as an organization of women's groups that collect shea kernels. The most important aspect for this group is the need to have a warehouse where they can store their produce, which will be sold at market price. A cooperative and signing contracts to sell their kernels, shea collectors can increase their profit by up to 50%. The project team has already met with the women's group to discuss the market price per bag rates from USD 15.72 to USD 23.81 and the profit per woman rises from USD 31.44 to USD 69.62. This annex is not in line with the latest responses to the economic analysis as referred above.

101. However, assuming that women receive additional income after project completion when selling their shea kernels at market price, the question remains whether they will be willing to invest the additional income to maintain the vast agroforestry landscape, including managing fire and grazing risks, without additional support from the private sector?

102. The project estimates that a USD 55.9 million investment will create income streams (throughout the life of the project) for women shea collectors estimated at USD 3,156,000.53. However, regarding the question from the independent TAP on the estimated income for the private sector from the project investments, the project team has already met with the private sector to discuss this issue every year. Considering a price of USD 4,000 per tonne for shea butter, USD290 per tonne for oleo and USD1,260 per tonne for shea nuts, it is clear that there are no markets in domestic or international markets. According to these numbers it seems that the income to communities of the project investments will not be sufficient.

103. Since the project will end up benefiting mostly the profits of the big industries, it is fair to question why GCF grant resources are needed to support profitable shea businesses. There could be a case for supporting small-scale farmers and their families with additional environmental benefits with greater equity. This might be achieved with fair shea prices and more remunerative income from other activities.

104. The resources from the Government of Ghana will mostly support the plantations of the MTS models in output 2 and a minor proportion of outputs 1, 4, and 5.

105. The project team has already met with the private sector to discuss revenues from REDD+ plus could sustain the activities initiated by the proposal. However, the project involves a small amount of resources to this end and the proposal does not present the true stage of advancement of the REDD+ pilot in Ghana.

106. Overall, the project should develop a much stronger case in terms of sustainability, value for money and equity. The private sector contributions are mainly from RECMAs, the shea cooperatives and the restored landscapes with more precise agreements with the private sector.

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The independent TAP recommends that the Board approve the project subject to the following:

- Prior to the second disbursement in respect of the project, the accredited entity shall submit to the Secretariat a sustainability report, in a form and substance satisfactory to the Secretariat.

- The financial sources and agreements to fund the CREMA funds during and after project completion.

- Develop an agreement with the Forestry Commission of Ghana with the private sector to use their committed resources and provide further support to sustain the shea landscapes after project completion; and

- An analysis should be conducted on the potential for income generation by communities compared with accurate costs for maintaining shea plantations, monitoring and management. The objective is to ensure that there are sufficient funds available to maintain sustainable productive landscapes in order to ensure that communities receive sufficient income to cover the costs plus additional profits to support better livelihoods. This analysis should be conducted as part of the sustainability assessment of the project interventions after project completion. This analysis should also consider partnering models with communities for revenue generation approach.

# Introduction

This gender assessment aims to provide an overview of the situation in Ghana, with a specific focus on the GLRBP proposal. It therefore identifies gender issues that are relevant to the proposal and examines how these can be better mainstreaming opportunities. The assessment was based on available data from studies conducted by the Government of Ghana, including the Forestry Commission, donor agencies, and multilateral development banks. Specifically, this action involved:

- a. Undertaking a desk review of existing gender analyses and gender and natural resource management (GNRM) research.
- b. Aligning approaches in this proposal with the national priorities of Ghana as provided in the roadmap developed by the Forestry Commission to guide the design and implementation of a gender strategy.
- c. Reviewing and considering national aspirations as detailed in the National Policy on gender, women's empowerment and social protection.
- d. Interacting with key stakeholders (men and women) living within the shea landscape;
- e. Incorporating information and lessons learned from past studies and assessments on gender in Ghana from the Government of Ghana, the United Nations, civil society organisations, and multilateral development banks.
- f. Integrating gender considerations in the project's indicators, targets and activities, and identifying women as leaders and decision-makers.

## Brief Overview of the Gender Dynamics of the Shea Landscape

Ghana’s forests are endowed with rich natural resources: gold, timber, non-timber forest products (NTFPs), etc. With a current rate of deforestation and forest degradation estimated at 3% annually for the last decade, Ghana is one of the countries in West Africa that faces pressure from agricultural expansion, logging, wood harvesting for charcoal production and firewood, mining, wineries and poaching. The rapid population growth and achievements have come at a significant cost to its forests. Today, over 60% of timber production since 1950 has been lost (27 million hectares). The future of Ghana’s forest is an issue of major concern. The project landscape consists of the Northern Region, Upper East Region, Upper West Region and parts of the Volta Region. It lies at the heart of the Shea Forests and contains some of the largest remaining tracts of forests and forest reserves. The landscape is mostly rural and the population is mainly agrarian and typically involved in small-scale subsistence and market farming as well as the main dominated cash crop production (shea nuts). Women are the primary producers, processors and traders. Women’s participation in agriculture is particularly higher during the farming season and post-harvest transportation, storage and marketing. Low soil fertility in many areas is encouraging communities to access fertilizers and other agrochemicals. However, these inputs are expensive and women cannot afford the cost of fertilisers and other agrochemicals. The increasing cost of agricultural inputs continue to threaten the little gains farmers make, negatively impacting household income. In response, women

## Existing Gender Inequality in Ghana

Although Ghana has seen an increase in its Human Development Index (HDI) value moving from 0.459 in 1990 to 0.579 in 2015, this progress, while significant, has not been universal and disparities exist between women and women and across social groups and geographical areas. Ghana’s HDI when adjusted for inequality (HDI-INEQUALITY) is lower than the national average of 0.648. This gap exists between a growing urban population that is benefiting from higher wages and improved access to services, and rural populations, particularly women who are still engaged in subsistence agriculture. Levels of development also vary geographically across Ghana’s 10 administrative regions with the highest levels of development found in the Western Region and the lowest in the North East, one of the poorest in the country and experiencing the slowest growth rates. Additionally, given historical patriarchal influences and socialization processes within the home and public settings, deeply rooted gender stereotypes continue to influence women’s roles and responsibilities. These factors disadvantage women and girls and range from inequality in access to social protection, education and skilled training, health care, economic opportunities, political participation and even macro-economic issues including trade, industry structures and productive resources.

More specifically, the National Gender Policy (2015), highlights the following key challenges and hurdles within the country in achieving gender equality and the empowerment of women and girls:

* **Inadequate access to social services:** This includes inadequate access to education, health care, skilled training gaps and excess maternal mortality.
* **Inequalities in the burden of extreme poverty, education, skilled training gaps and excess maternal mortality.**
* **Unequal access to social, economic power and justice including lack of respect for and inadequate protection and promotion of human rights of women and girls.**
* **Women’s limited participation in decision-making at all levels and in dealing with all kinds of conflicts, in securities and threats on women and girls.**
* **Inequalities in macro-economic issues including trade, industry structures and productive resources.**

Strengthening and persistent discrimination against women and girls that manifest in negative gender stereotypes, social norms and practices continue to be a major challenge for maternal health and mortality.

Concluding remark: The policy seeks to address the impact of these inequalities by improving the legal environment through enactment of statutes to promoting girl-child education and skill training for young women. It was in this vein that the National Gender Policy was also created in 2015 with the

## 3.1 Gender Inequality Index

Through the years, several indices have developed to quantify the concept of gender inequality. The United Nations Development Programme uses the Gender Inequality Index (GII) and Gender Development Index (GGDI). GII is a composite index that measures the gap in achievements between women and men in three areas: reproductive health, empowerment, and the market with a focus on women's achievement in human development in these three areas: health, education, and command over economic resources. The GGI considers the gender gaps on human development between men and women.

Ghana has a GII of 0.547 in the 2015 rankings and ranks 131 out of 188 countries assessed. The GDI value is 0.69, which means that Ghana's index measures inequality in human-development Index achievements between men and women.

The Global Gender Gap Index (GGGI) of the World Economic Forum examines the gap between men and women in four categories: economic participation and opportunity, educational attainment, health survival, and political empowerment." Out of 144 countries, Ghana rankings, based on GGGI in the year 2016 are given below:

| Dimension | Score | Rank |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Economic participation and opportunity | 0.805 | 10 |
| Educational attainment | 0.931 | 119 |
| Health survival | 0.977 | 85 |
| Political empowerment | 0.112 | 95 |
| Gender Gap Index 2016 | 0.705 | 59 |

* Inequality = 0.00; Equality = 1.00: Source: The Global Gender Gap Report 2014

The Organization for Economic Cooperation and Development (OECD) developed the Social Institutions and Gender Index (SIGI), a composite index that scores countries (i.e., 0 to 1 on 14 indicators grouped into five sub-indexes: discriminatory family code, restricted physical integrity, son bias, restricted resources and assets, and restricted civil liberties) to measure the discrimination against

## 3.2 Poverty

The Ghana Living Standards Survey (GLSS) reports have provided considerable data on poverty in Ghana and the GLSS 5 and 6 have indicated that a quarter of Ghanaians are poor while less than 10% are extremely poor. The poverty is intense in the rural savanna including the shea landscape with more than a quarter of households living below the poverty line (GLSS 2013). In the Upper West region, overall, the dynamics of poverty in Ghana over the period 2005/06 to 2012/13 indicate that poverty is still very much a rural phenomenon and there are regional variations, with the Upper West region being the most affected. The GLSS data show that poverty rates have increased across all periods (Cooke et al., 2016). Both regions lie within the shea landscape. Ultimately, the poverty gap ratio is highest in the northern savanna and lowest in the northern rainforest and savannah regions and this has implications for women and men, girls and boys.

In terms of distribution of wealth by quintiles, women have a greater share of wealth in the poorest quintiles and the reverse is also true. It is also estimated that average hourly earnings of women in Ghana is 20% lower than those of men (World Bank, 2015). Women are more likely to be poor, earn less than men even when, on average, they work longer hours.

Female-headed households have been flagged as a special concern since, not only do they face the double burden of unpaid domestic and productive work, but also responsibility and growing vulnerability. However, to tackle female vulnerability to poverty goes beyond female headed households and must focus on the individual. Addressing intra-household gender differences is crucial as a fundamental way of improving women’s income and economic security (Ambala, 2006).

## 3.3 Education

The Ministry of Education, its agencies and stakeholders have put strategies in place to deal with existing and future challenges of gender in the sector. Notable among these is the Education Strategic Plan, 2010 – 2020, which has placed gender equality and women’s and girls’ empowerment concerns, such as improving access at education levels, particularly the basic level, as well as the use of female role models, among others.

Gender differences in enrolment for formal education have narrowed slightly in the recent past but continue to persist particularly at higher levels. Drop-out rates for boys and literacy rates are still much lower than those of girls. The gender gap is also evident in access to secondary school education which is beneficial use of limited funds. The cost of education keeps rising putting many families under financial strain which is evidenced by continued high drop-out rates. The current rate for girls (46 per cent) is considered to be a major concern and the Ministry of Education has indicated that it will consider that the recent national policy directive launched by the government to grant free education to the high school level may improve this situation. The continuing gender imbalance in access to education limits women’s access to employment and productivity. In urban areas, lack of qualifications and narrow
