## A. Political participation and decision-making

Since the promulgation of Ghana’s multi-party democracy, the country has maintained continuous progress towards a more inclusive democratic system. The Constitution establishes the following branches with relative division of power: The Executive, Council of State; Legislature; and Judiciary.

The Affirmative Action Policy, formulated in 1998 by the Government, after the Beijing Plan of Action, sets targets for increasing women’s representation at all levels of government including Ministries, Boards, Commissions, Councils and Official Boards including Cabinet and Council of State.

And although the Constitution guarantees all persons the opportunity to participate in decision-making at every level, socio-cultural structures and traditional practices still hinder women's participation in the spheres of political and public life. Even though women occupy very important positions within the government, they are not adequately represented in the legislative branch. Thus only 12.7% of legislators are women. There are only 2 women legislators out of 57 in the Northern (31), Upper East (4) and Upper West(10) regions which constitute the northern region. At the district assembly, there is distinctly a lack of women appointed by the President of Ghana. They are political appointees and an extension of the executive branch of government. Within the Northern Region, 4 out of 25 District Assemblies; in the Upper East Region, 1 out of 3 women and in the Upper West Region, 3 out of 11.

According to Ghana’s Millennium Development Goals (MDGs) Report published in 2011, in the Judiciary, only 29% of the Supreme Court judges and 25% of High Court judges are women. In the Civil Service, 47% of the Permanent Secretaries are women; however, only 38% of Deputy Directors General and 37% Municipal Chief Executives (MCEs) and District Chief Executives (DCEs), representing 8.2%, were women. In Parliament (2013), the proportion of women is 19.5%. whereas in the Sub-Saharan region the average is 16%.

There have been some initiatives targeted at promoting the participation of women in public life with the development of the Women’s Manifesto starting in 2003. There have also been other activities to increase votes for female candidates to parliament, but as these statistics illustrate, in terms of real representation and decision-making power, women are still underrepresented.

These inequities are due in part to the fact that, traditionally Ghanaian women are effectively under the control or authority of men (initially their father or other male members of their kin group and later their husband) within the family structure. Although they may gain status and influence with age. As a result, women’s decision-making role in Ghana is constrained in both private and public spheres, markedly so in the northern regions.

## 3.5 Labour Force Participation

The 2010 National Population and Housing Census (NPC) (Ghana Statistical Service, 2013) indicates that the proportion of employed males is slightly higher than the proportion of females employed except in services and sales (31.4% female, 10.2% male) and elementary occupations (7.5% female, 4.6% male). The NPC also shows that women are more likely to be self-employed or own their own business compared with men, indicating that the proportion of females (31.1%) engaged as service and sales workers is more than three times the proportion of their male counterparts (9.2%). In support of these data, the 2010 PRC shows that of the employed women in Ghana, 45% are self-employed or own their own business compared with only 37% for men.

For females, the principal employment sectors include agriculture, forestry and fishing; wholesale and retail trade; transport and storage; and domestic services. For males, the principal employment sectors include agriculture, forestry and fishing; manufacturing; construction; wholesale and retail trade; repair of motor vehicles and motorcycles; and 8.8% in manufacturing. For females, the proportion employed in agricultural activities is higher than for men (51.7% compared with 40%). Women are also more likely to be engaged in informal employment where poverty is low, it is highly dominated by men; and women far outnumber men in non-farm self-employment and private enterprises. Women earn a lower income from their work.

In effect, gender dimensions of poverty are likely to be biased against women compared with feminization of poverty.

Within the shea landscape, for generations women have exclusively harvested and processed shea nuts into butter often under rough-and-unforgiving circumstances. What is gathered is then processed into nuts and sold on the market or to cooperatives for processing into shea butter. It is suggested that Ghana is among the top exporters of shea butter in Africa and produces 130,000 tons per year.

In rural Ghana, shea butter extraction is a common activity among women and it is a female-dominated occupation. The process involves collecting shea nuts from trees which are located far away five days of manual labour, for example, the female farmer may earn only about US$2, from selling her harvest to a cooperative or middleman who then sells the nuts on to a processing plant. Shea butter is produced on a first come basis. It is often the case that the trees are located for the home and women must face long distances in order to collect shea nuts. The process of collecting shea nuts involves walking through thick vegetation, climbing trees, and cutting branches from which the shea nuts hang. They are also not protected from the heat, rough terrain and poisonous snakes they face while collecting nuts. Increasing the number of easily accessible shea trees increase the quantity of shea that women can collect in a given time period.

The processing of shea nuts into butter is a two-stage process which involves roasting the nuts to remove the shell and then grinding them with a mortar and pestle or by hand. The roasted nuts are then placed on a wooden board where they are crushed using a pestle and mortar. The resulting paste is then put in a cloth bag, and the shea butter is extracted from it by squeezing the bag. The process of extracting the shea butter can be industrialized which produces a better quality and requires sophisticated skills and expertise. The effects of this on the women need to be taken into account as processing becomes more mechanized and the nuts are ground and processed in factories. The entire value chain is seen to be beneficial. Additionally, seasonal fluctuations in the supply of shea nuts often affects prices. This means that women who have invested time and effort in collecting and processing shea nuts due to the short term need for income, women are often forced to sell at low prices and sell their stock at a loss. It is estimated that as a result some intermediaries are profiting by margins of 300-500%.


---

# Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Action Plan

GSREP GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL

According to a study in Northern Ghana[1], 90% of women view shea as the major source of their livelihoods. Despite shea importance for women, there are substantial challenges to the ability of women shea collectors to earn a sufficient share of the value chain income generation:

- Lack of market power – Many women shea collectors are operating at an individual, small scale level and lack access to markets that can provide large quantities of kernels to create direct supply chains and move up in the value chain.
- Lack of agency in the community – Less than 10% of women farmers in Ghana own land. Many women have no say over decisions about how their land is used for production, marketing, or land management, although these decisions directly impact their income-generating activities (See next section).

To help the women gain more from the shea value chain, to make efficiency gains and earn fairer wages, a number of cooperatives are being formed to enable better business practices and benefits. The international NGO AROCHA for example, is working with communities to improve their age-old harvesting methods and marketing strategies. In Ghana, several private sector companies are helping to empower the women and improve their economic standing. Similarly, SNV, a not-for-profit international development organization, has partnered with the women’s cooperatives in cocoa and beauty manufacture, partnered with the women’s cooperatives in Ghana, to address the challenges discussed above. A joint venture was formed: a bulk purchase agreement with the women cooperatives, guaranteed access for market for their shea butter and shea nuts. This bulk purchase contract guards against heavy price fluctuations, and as a result many women are now making a reliable income, improving their family lives and building a sustainable future from the shea nut and butter business.[2]

## 3.6 Access to resources

The 1992 Constitution vested all land in the People in trust for the people of Ghana and divided it into two categories under held by public and customary tenure systems. Within these systems, land ownership and land security varies widely across regions. However, it generally accords men the exclusive property right to land excluding women. Women have access to land mainly through their husbands or members of the family, whereas they might be allocated plots or cultivate their husband’s fields.[3]

With the aim of improving land administration and security of tenure through systematic land titling and development of a national land information system (NLIS), and in order to help reduce poverty, the Ministry of Lands and Natural Resources (MLNR) in 2003 initiated the first phase of the Land Administration Project (LAP) at the national level. In response to national gender initiatives and demands for gender equality in land administration, the MLNR and its partners developed the Project took steps to gain gender dimensions of land administration development. It developed a gender equality mainstreaming strategy that was adopted by the MLNR in 2013. The LAP has been designed to address women’s and men’s concerns in land administration for equitable development, gathering gender information on land tenure and land use, and promoting gender equality. Key aspects of the strategy include Public Education, Capacity Building, Institutional Reforms, Advocacy and Networking with Civil Society Organizations (CSOs), and Monitoring and Evaluation. The LAP was implemented from 2013 to 2015 under the Second Phase of LAP with funding from the World Bank, Department of Foreign Affairs.

## Trade and Development (SDAT), Canada formerly CIDA, and the Government of Ghana.

The Ministry has integrated gender perspectives in LAP planning and ensures adequate women’s representation and involvement in decision making on land. It is also promoting women's access to and control over land and other natural resources through the implementation of this project implementation to inform policy reforms in the land sector and administration in Ghana.


However, many women are not aware of the existence of various land laws and of the guarantees they can provide them. Even when women are aware about them, they often lack support in the process of claiming their rights. Women also face a number of other barriers that prevent them from fully exercising their rights to land. For example, women cannot own livestock or crops because they can only cultivate food crops and not cash crops. Given existing gender inequalities and social, local and cultural norms, women are often excluded from decision making in the household. They make most decisions on land issues. If women do participate, they are generally limiters or resource persons. Women also face a number of other barriers that prevent them from fully exercising their rights to land. They may, however, be granted access by chiefs to farm on the land and would usually be obliged to assist the husband to farm on his land and when time permits, go to farm on her own lands too.


Given these barriers, women’s access to bank loans through formal channels is more limited than that of men. Women are also less likely to have collateral for a loan or other assets that can secure the credit. The majority of female farmers obtain capital from informal sources, including loans from household members and friends. However, because they lack collateral and other assets, women face a physical difficulty in accessing credit because financial institutions are situated in urban areas and transaction costs are too high. Given that extension services usually target men as heads of the households rural women also have less access than men to training and technical information.


## 3.7 Health

According to Ghana’s 2010 Population and Housing Census Report on Mortality, life expectancy at birth for males is 59.4 years and for females it is 64.4 years. The Maternal Mortality Ratio in Ghana, at the national level, is 485 deaths per 100,000 live births. The Infant Mortality Rate, per 1,000 live births, is 63 deaths per 1,000 live births.


The main gender issues related health deal with overall low levels of expenditure and provision as well as the lack of priority accorded to women’s health in its own right. There are also biases in expenditure towards urban, curative facilities and negative effects of recent cost recovery policies on certain social groups.

The Ministry of Health developed a Gender Policy in 2009 to direct its actions on handling these gender related health concerns. Maternal mortality has been one of the major areas of health inequity, but the Ghana MDD report (2013) indicates that the situation has improved because a number of interventions have been put into place and more women are accessing maternal health services including personnel. Most other challenges are related to poverty. The National Maternal Control Programme (NMCP) was launched in 2004 with the aim of reducing the high levels of maternal morbidity with high incidence in women. A campaign was started in the Northern Region encouraging the use of insecticide treated nets and which targeted children under six years and pregnant women.

# Annex 8 - Gender Assessment and Action Plan

GSREP GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL

HIV and AIDS is another health condition that has higher incidence in women more than men in Ghana but the incidence and prevalence has been reducing because of the concerted efforts of the Government of Ghana, partners and other stakeholders. According to the National HIV/AIDS and Malaria Estimates Report, an estimated 225,478 people were living with HIV and AIDS out of which 100,369 (44%) are women, 125,109 (56%) men and 1,000 (0.4%) children. The prevalence rate amongst pregnant women attending antenatal clinic in 2016 was 2.4%. HIV prevalence among the young population (15-24 years), a proxy for new infections, remained unchanged at 3.1%. The highest prevalence of HIV infection is found in the Western region where it stands at 7.8% while the national prevalence ranged from 2.7% in the Volta Region to 0.76% in the northern region (project region). The other project regions, Upper East and Upper West were, respectively, at 1.53 and 1.59%.

Women are disproportionately vulnerable to HIV/AIDS and other STIs because of limits on their control over their sexual relationships, which is a result of lack of access to or control of extensive sexual networking via male partners through polygamy and/or promiscuous relationships. Women's control of their sexuality is also limited by cultural norms and social values that are gendered imperatives which push some women into sexual exchange for survival, and make women dependent on men for economic security and other forms of support. Strengthening women’s control over their sexuality by increasing their bargaining power within relationships, and giving them wider economic choices is the key to reducing their risk of infection in the longer term.

## 4. Legal and Administrative Framework Protecting Women and Protecting Gender Equality

### 4.1 Key International Protocols and Treaties Ratified by Ghana in Support of Gender Equality and Women's Empowerment

Ghana has ratified many key international conventions, treaties, and plans of action on gender equality and women’s empowerment. Critical among them are:

- The Convention on the Rights of the Child (CRC) 1989
- The Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) 1979 (Signed 17 July 1980 and Ratified 2 January 1986);
- The Optional Protocol to the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) 1999 (Signed 24 February 2000);
- International Covenant on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights (ICESCR) 1966 (Ratified 7 December 1983)
- The Covenant on Economic Social and Cultural Rights (ECSOCR): The Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action of 1995; The Vienna Declaration and Programme of Action of 1993;
- The African Charter on Human and Peoples’ Rights (ACHPR) (Ratified the 24 January 1989 and Signed 3 July 2004);
- Protocol to the African Charter on Human and People’s Rights on the rights of women in Africa

## Key National Gender Equality and Women’s Empowerment Frameworks, Policies and Laws

The National Gender Policy (2015) is Ghana's main policy for promoting gender equality and women's empowerment. Its goal is to mainstream gender equality and women's empowerment into the national development agenda in order to address the social, economic, socio-economic, cultural issues, health and agriculture, trade and related matters.

- Accelerate efforts and commitments of government in empowering women (especially women with disability) to have safe and secure livelihoods, access to economic opportunities, decent work, education, health care, housing, food security, culture, socio-economic and cultural issues, health and agriculture, trade and related matters.
- Speed up enforcement and domestication of ratified international treaties, policies and strategies on gender equality and women's empowerment in order to achieve national gender equality and women's empowerment nationwide;
- Support the implementation of the National Action Plan and put in place transformative measures (including leadership development) that will enable women and men to have equal rights in achieving at least the 40% women representation in politics, on Boards and at all levels.
- Improve women’s economic opportunities including engendering macro-economic and trade policies, promoting women's access to credit and other financial services;
- Transform inequitable gender relations in order to improve women's status relative to that of men.

Additionally, as highlighted by the National Gender Policy, there are also other national laws, policies and legislative frameworks which also support gender equality and women’s empowerment in Ghana. See table below.

## 4.3 Key Development Frameworks, Plans and Actions Promoting Gender Equality and Women Empowerment

Apart from the constitutional, legislative and key national legal frameworks for gender equality and women’s empowerment noted above, there are other existing national frameworks contributing to gender equality and women's empowerment within the country. These key frameworks and plans are listed below:

- The National Medium Term Development Plans have all outlined strategies to deal with gender equality and women empowerment issues.
- For example, (within the Ghana Poverty Reduction Strategy 2013 – 2017; Gender Equality Resolution II 2005; GOGGA I (2009) – III (2014); GGGDA II (2014 – 2017).
- An Affirmative Action Policy of 1998 calls for a 40% quota of women representation on all government and public boards, commissions, councils, committees and other offices, including Cabinet and the State of Council.
- All policy frameworks that manifest that issue include gender and special emphasis on policy aspects for eliminating discrimination and violence against women and girls; recruiting girls from early marriages and motherhood; and engagement of men to enable women actualize their rights.
- The Ministry of Food and Agriculture (MOFA) in 2001 developed a Gender and Agricultural Development Strategy (GADS) to support its gender mainstreaming processes.
- Ministry of Health (MoH) in 2009 developed a sector policy on handling gender equality issues in the health care system.
- The Ghana AIDS Commission in February 2015, developed a National HIV and AIDS, STI Policy that has provisions for gender equality and women’s work.
- National Health Insurance Scheme (NHIS) has been instituted to facilitate the provision of affordable health care for all citizens. Children under 18 years whose parent(s) or guardian(s)

## Institutional Arrangements

As described in the Ghana’s Gender Policy document, Ghana has a functioning Department of Gender with regional units that work under the Ministry of Gender, Children and Social Protection (MoGCSP). The Department of Gender was called the National Council on Women and Development (NCWD) when it was established by Act 208 of 1975. In 1994, during the Second National Conference on Women and Girls in Africa, the NCWD became part of the Office of the President and operated with linkages to relevant Ministries, Departments and Agencies (MDAs). It created the Gender Desk Officers (GDOs) in all MDAs in order to ensure that gender issues were mainstreamed into their work. The MoGCSP was established ensuring that gender concerns were incorporated into sector policies, plans and programmes of MDAs. In 2001, the government established a fully-fledged sector ministry, the Ministry for Women and Children Affairs (MoWCA). The MoGCSP was renamed the Department of Gender in 2003 as a stand-alone instrument. The role of the ministry was further expanded in January 2013, when it was renamed the Ministry of Gender, Children and Social Protection where social protection and welfare has been added within its mandate to include child protection, women’s empowerment, gender equality and women’s empowerment. In summary, the current national machinery of Ghana that oversees gender equality and women’s empowerment issues involves the following:

- MoGCSP
- National Advisory Board to the MoGCSP
- Department of Gender
- Department of Children
- Department of Social Welfare
- Department of Social Protection
- Human Trafficking Secretariat
- Domestic Violence Secretariat
- Regional Directors of Social Welfare, Children and Gender Departments
- District offices of Department of Social Welfare

### Gender Desk Officer at Metropolitan, Municipal and District Assembly (MMDA)

Additionally, Ghana established a national REDD+ Gender Sub-Committee Group (GSWG) in 2015 with financial support from the UNDP through the facilitation of the GCF/UNDP Joint Secretariat, as well as the FCPF and the National REDD+ Secretariat under the existing REDD+ funding to Ghana. Including representatives from government, traditional authorities, local communities, academia, private sector and non-governmental organizations, this group was established with a mandate to support the gender mainstreaming process, building capacity on gender at the grassroots level, and providing technical assistance in the implementation of projects and programmes that address gender responsiveness. To support this work, the GSWG, at the time of its formation, was also tasked on climate change, REDD+ and its status in Ghana, the links between gender, REDD+, and safeguard issues, and the importance of mainstreaming gender considerations into REDD+.

## 5. Gender Issues Around Forests and REDD+

In Ghana, there are gender differentiated use, access and control of forest resources. It is often the case that women have more access to and control over forests, reserve areas and sacred groves, while women go to the edge of the forest, the productive area for wild fruits as well as the deep areas, for herbal medicines. Similarly, men are more likely to be involved in extracting timber for commercial purposes or for construction materials, charcoal production, and firewood collection, where women typically gather forest products for fuel, fencing, food for the family, fodder for livestock and raw material for making crafts such as thatch and charcoal burning. For the most part, forests have been acknowledged to be the domain of the men.

In most parts of Ghana, women do usually have user rights when they obtain benefits such as forest products (fuel wood, charcoal production, fruits of dawadawa and shea nut picking and food wrapping leaves) from forests. However, income in forests that are under communal ownership by communities and traditional authorities

Presented below are additional (negotiable/societal culture norms, challenges and risks faced by women in Ghana around REDD+ actions:

- Lack of knowledge and awareness on forest laws
- Limited access to empowerment for women to seek justice on land issues in the court.
- Lack of capacity among women to be involved in forest management programmes, partly due to their low levels of education and literacy.
- Small land holdings among women.
- Cultural practices and patriarchal values wherein men often take charge and the women are left out.
- Given the multiple roles taken on, they often do not have enough time to get as involved with forest management programmes as men, and as such may not benefit equally.
- Since women’s access to land is low, their participation in forest management programmes is also often minimal.

Given these gender disparities around socio-cultural norms and legal inequalities and impediments, the dynamics of existing customary and statutory laws on marriage, divorce, inheritance, custody and land tenure, women's access to resources is limited. This limits their ability to meet their basic needs and will thereby have an impact on the Project activities, in areas of inclusive participation, rights, equity and benefit sharing. Women’s contribution to the forestry sector and their critical role as major stakeholders in forest management are not fully recognized. This has a direct bearing on women's ability to reap adequate benefits from the forests, as well as the optimisation of their potential as powerful agents of change in society. The project therefore strongly sets up the basis to foster gender equality and incorporate gender considerations into the project activities.


## 6. Gender integration within REDD+ Action Plan


Through collaboration efforts between Ghana Forestry Commission and other partners, a Gender and REDD+ Roadmap was drafted in 2011. To inform its development, participatory multi-stakeholder workshops were held, which convened national policy makers, women’s organizations and gender experts, among others. These workshops focused on building participants’ capacity on gender sensitive approaches to REDD+. The Roadmap is a guide for gender mainstreaming within the REDD+ process and could support gender-sensitive national REDD+ processes. The Roadmap has served as a guide on gender for other REDD+ processes, including the Environmental and Social Assessment and later the 2013 National Action Plan (NAP). The Roadmap is also a tool to help women in Ghana gain access to participate in, contribute to, and benefit from various REDD+ programs, policies and funds, and hence, contribute to environmental and social sustainability. Its goal is to help to ensure that Ghana’s REDD+ policy development and implementation is gender sensitive and meets international safeguards standards.


The Gender and REDD+ Roadmap affirms that the Government of Ghana is cognizant of the huge contribution of forests to the income/ livelihoods of rural women, and the different roles men and women play in forest management. It also recognizes that there are significant gender differences exist with respect to decision-making, participation, ownership, access and control of resources such as land, as well as equity in the sharing of benefits associated with the forest. In response to these challenges, the Roadmap outlines a series of actions aimed at empowering women to minimize and/or reduce negative impacts of actions on the disadvantaged/vulnerable groups in society, particularly women. The Roadmap also supports the country's commitment to national and international commitments to gender equality and to break down the gendered border on gender equality in relation to women’s access and control over productive resources.


Listed below are key actions outlined for Phase 1 and Phase 2 within the Gender and REDD+ Roadmap.

* Recognise women as key forest stakeholders and harness the roles of both men and women;
* Establish and support organisations and networks to work on gender and forest-related issues;
* Empower and build the capacity of women and men on gender issues;
* Ensure that women’s voices are heard in consultations, decision-making participation of women in negotiations and in the design and implementation of demonstration activities (pilot projects);
* Support the mainstreaming of gender into policy reforms associated with REDD+;
* Strongly encourage and promote the collection of sex-disaggregated data throughout the REDD+ process and the inclusion of gender analyses for the use of, access to, and control of forest resources;
* Foster a partnership with the media for effective information sharing of practices that address women’s needs;
* Develop safeguards to ensure women’s rights to land and natural resource use, and ensure that these are clearly communicated in REDD+ projects comply with international safeguard standards;
* Incorporate grievance mechanisms that establish and support legal protection of the rights of women on an equal basis with those of men.

## Phase 2:

* Enable full and effective consultation and participation of women and men in all stages of the REDD+ process;
* Provide women and men with equal access to information regarding all aspects of REDD+;
* Build the capacity of women and men to enable inclusive decision-making and informed opinion;
* Involve women in all monitoring, reporting and verification (MRV) and monitoring and evaluation activities, and provide the necessary tools and knowledge needed for women to engage meaningfully;
* Ensure that women have the option to participate in all types of formal and non-formal training programmes related to REDD+ and forest management and benefit from them fully;
* Ensure the equitable distribution of benefits; paying special attention to traditional practices that entrench inequality and could exclude women from participating in all activities and/or having access to resources;
* Design and implement gender-sensitive benefit sharing systems that ensure women are provided with fair and appropriate compensation, incentives, benefits and revenues associated with REDD+;
* Design and implement a gender-responsive monitoring and evaluation system;
* Create opportunities for training and capacity building for girls and women to be more actively involved in technical aspects of forest management.

In addition to the Roadmap and as part of Ghana’s REDD+ readiness process, funded by the Forest Carbon Partnership Fund, a Strategic Environmental and Social Assessment (SESA) of the REDD+ mechanism was conducted. The SESA identified key gender issues that need to be addressed for on-going readiness activities, the findings, objectives and actions of the 2011 Gender and REDD+ Roadmap, and the 2013-2015 SESA Action Plan. In its design and implementation, it uses its frameworks for REDD+ with special consideration on livelihoods, land rights, health, gender, vulnerable groups, governance, capacity building and biodiversity. ### 7. Stakeholder Engagement

This gender analyst, undertaken at the onset and design of this project, acts as an entry point for gender mainstreaming throughout design and implementation phases. The analysis was complimented by stakeholder consultations which took place from August to December 2013. These were specifically to the recommendations and provide information for the development of the Gender Action Plan. A summary of the results from the consultations are detailed below in the Stakeholder Engagement section (for a larger summary of the stakeholder engagement work, please see Annex VII).


#### Stakeholder engagement efforts

Consultations with various women, men, youth and women's groups took place from the 23 to 25 August 2013 in four communities in the project landscape in the northern regions of Ghana. The target population were women on the fringes of forest reserves and therefore dependent on the forest for daily sustenance.

Corroboration to the findings of this gender assessment described above emerged from the stakeholder consultations as follows:

- At the community level, women are disadvantaged in terms of access and control of resources and ownership such as land;
- At the household level; women often do not own anything (sheep, cattle etc);
- No evidence of empowerment among women to participate fully in community projects;
- Women are very committed to the community cause and usually enjoy support from the men, which they did not case previously;
- In some communities, women seem confident enough to play lead roles if/when given the opportunity;
- There is some fear that men may take over some aspects of women's traditional role/activities like shea nut picking if it becomes very 'commercialised' and begins to earn good money. Specific concerns are that women have training and access to any new technology needs to be introduced by the project;
- Personal health, safety and security concerns during the harvesting of nuts in the forest e.g., carry heavy loads, risk of snake bites, etc.
- Youth interest is mostly on immediate results and financial returns rather than sacrificing for

### Recommendations for project design and implementation

The analysis and findings above have been considered throughout the on-going development process of the proposed project and have helped to formulate recommendations and illustrate key entry points for gender action within it as well.

Key entry points and recommendations for gender action within project design and implementation are listed below:

- **Build capacity of women and provide them with adequate resources (e.g. technology, finance) in order to enable their participation in the project**
- **Ensure efforts to build capacity of stakeholders and project beneficiaries are gender-responsive and equitably engage women and men (and youth, when applicable).**
- **Promote gender equality in all aspects of the project design, implementation, monitoring and structures;**
- **Develop specific activities and strategies in the project to include / target women and female-headed households (with defined targets).**
- **Develop a gender analysis as part of the environmental and social impact assessment (ESIA), in which field-based data-collection and consultations take place in order to conduct a contextualized gender analysis at the local level. In addition, the need for additional gender-sensitive and sex-disaggregated data on women’s access to resources, decision-making power, participation in public life and responsibilities in communities, violence against women, etc., should also be assessed and any assumptions made during the ESIA is necessary.**
- **Gender and also adequate funding and expertise for implementing gender-related strategies, monitoring the results of implementation, and holding individuals and institutions accountable for outcomes that promote gender equality are required.**
- **Utilize existing gender policies and guidelines to integrate them into the management structures of the project.**
- **Advocate and raise awareness on gender and REDD+ among project and government staff involved in the design and implementation of the project.**
- **Include a Gender Specialist position / provision of advice within the project to implement gender-related strategies.**
- **During project implementation conduct qualitative assessments on the gender-specific benefits that can be directly associated to the project.**
- **Women’s organizations should also be included as partners for the project to take steps to address their specific concerns, integrate their perspectives and ensure they benefit from the project.**

It should be noted that these recommendations above are based on preliminary findings of this assessment and a desk review. ## 9. Gender Action Plan

Data presented above provides context and an overall baseline assessment on the gender dynamics, inequalities and state-of-play within Ghana, its forest sector and its effects on REDD+ action to date. This analysis identified the differences between men and women and has helped to identify gaps and provide a basis for developing a gender action plan. The plan will be used as a monitoring and evaluation tool in order to monitor or evaluate progress on gender equality and women’s empowerment within the project.

In this process, quantitative and qualitative gender-sensitive indicators have been developed and sex- and age-disaggregated data will be collected, with the goal of measuring both the numerical impact and benefits of the project on women, men and youth as well as the changes in women's and men's (and youth, when applicable) perception and livelihoods as a result of the project. Increased income however is not the only indicator that will be used to measure success. Other indicators include the price that the cooperatives has sold their shea to the buyers. Increase in income is the difference between these two prices as well any other activity that the cooperative has chosen to undertake as a cooperative. The gender action plan will also provide an opportunity for successes in gender mainstreaming as well as areas of improvement, and allow the project to monitor changes and gender impacts in order to have them then feed back into decision-making and planning.

## Proposed gender targets to implement recommendations

Given the cross-cutting nature of the recommendations discussed above, gender-responsive activities will be integrated across the outputs of the proposed project. Women will be targeted as a key stakeholder group for all aspects of the project. To achieve this, the project has established the following 8 targets on gender:

| Target | Description |
| --- | --- |
| Target 1: Build capacity of women and provide them with economic assets (e.g., embankments, knowledge, etc.) to play a key role in decision-making at the value chain level. |
| Target 2: Rights to control access, manage, harvest and market forest products and associated resources are strengthened for women and youth. |
| Target 3: Women and men (and youth, when relevant) are meaningfully engaged in all stakeholder forums and awareness raising activities and decision-making processes. |
| Target 4: Women and men have equal access to information and through the coordinating body, committees, consultations, workshop, etc. |
| Target 5: The project will provide women with built capacities on how to mainstream gender in REDD+ implementation among involved agencies (e.g., FCI, REDD+ institutions and structures, and policy makers). |
| Target 6: Ghana’s Gender Sub-Working Group is integrated into the institutional structure for the project and is on the PSC. |
| Target 7: Resources for implementing gender-related activities are allocated |
| Target 8: A gender perspective is integrated into data collection, monitoring and reporting |
